\fname@algorithm
1

Abstract




LieDetect: Detection of representation orbits of

compact Lie groups from point clouds

Henrique Ennes
∗

Raphaël Tinarrage
†

August 9, 2026

∗†
FGV EMAp, Rio de Janeiro, Brazil

∗

https://github.com/HLovisiEnnes

†

https://raphaeltinarrage.github.io/

Abstract.

We suggest a new algorithm to estimate representations of compact Lie groups from finite samples of their orbits.
Different from other reported techniques, our method allows the retrieval of the precise representation type as a direct sum of irreducible representations. Moreover, the knowledge of the representation type permits the reconstruction of its orbit, which is useful to identify the Lie group that generates the action.
Our algorithm is general for any compact Lie group, but only instantiations for
\mathrm{SO}(2)
,
T^{d}
,
\mathrm{SU}(2)
, and
\mathrm{SO}(3)
are considered.
Theoretical guarantees of robustness in terms of Hausdorff and Wasserstein distances are derived.
Our tools are drawn from geometric measure theory, computational geometry, and optimization on matrix manifolds. The algorithm is tested for synthetic data up to dimension
16
, as well as real-life applications in image analysis, harmonic analysis, and classical mechanics systems, achieving very accurate results.

Keywords.

Computational geometry
\cdot

Sensitivity analysis for optimization problems on
manifolds
\cdot

Matrix Lie algebras
\cdot

Optimal transportation
\cdot

Geometric measure and integration theory, integral and normal currents in optimization
\cdot

Artificial neural networks and deep learning

MSC2020 codes.

68U05
\cdot

49Q12
\cdot

15B30
\cdot

49Q22
\cdot

49Q15
\cdot

68T07

Contents

1
Introduction

Motivation.

Arguably, the most impactful realization in early last-century quantitative sciences was the combination of the loose notions of symmetry with the precise definition of algebraic groups. From important results in cryptography
[
40
]
, passing through the description of the hydrogen atom
[
91
]
and the standard model of particles
[
41
]
, arriving at the formalization of Klein’s
Erlanger
program
[
30
]
, the group-theoretic point of view on symmetries allowed for unprecedented developments in many theoretical and practical fields. Therefore, it is unsurprising that the prominent role assumed by symmetries translated itself to the description of learning, be it natural—e.g., the detection of invariances in visual psychology
[
48
,
47
]
—or artificial.
Indeed, the ability to identify that a rotated ‘7’-digit is still a 7 tells us much about the important presence of symmetries—and, consequently, of groups—in the process of ‘understanding’.

Notice that the symmetries are not restricted to the cognition part of the process—i.e., they are not only devices created by our brain to simplify its training task, although they can be, sometimes, artificially-induced—, but they are inherited from the learning data. It is because we have seen many images of the same object rotated around its center or shifted in space and have been told, in whatever form, that these correspond to the same entity, that we acquired the invariance of the digit ‘7’ with respect to rotations. In a nutshell, this indicates that there is a genuine interest to identify, directly from data, the existence of symmetries on it, something which we review below. Whether and how the information of existing symmetries is used to improve the training process (which it does, as, again, we will review) is not the focus here; rather, we are primarily interested in the ability to tell, from data only, the existence of groups symmetries, their specifications, and the ‘symmetry’ types. Although not the focus, we give some taste of how this knowledge can improve Machine Learning algorithms in Section
6
.

We attempt to tackle the symmetry identification problem algorithmically.
That is, in a more mathematical language, our goal is to create a technique to determine the actions of groups that generate the symmetries on a data set. Of course, as stated, this task is rather open, so we assume some constraints.
The first of these is related to the algebraic groups considered. Instead of an algorithm that works for the whole class of groups, we will focus on detecting symmetries generated by
Lie groups
, those admitting smooth structures within their operations. In fact, the set of all Lie groups is still too big, so we will concentrate only on
compact
Lie groups, for reasons that will become clear throughout the text.

We will also restrict the kinds of symmetry, or in group language, actions, that describe how the Lie groups interact with datasets.
Instead of the general scenario, we consider only
representations
, which are linear actions of Lie groups in vector spaces. This means that only one particular kind of data set, the point clouds living in some
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, will be of any interest to us.
More precisely, we study point clouds
X
that are sampled on or close to a submanifold
\mathcal{O}
that carry a transitive action of its symmetry group.
Equivalently,
\mathcal{O}
is the
orbit
of a compact Lie group representation.
Moreover, it is always possible to embed such Lie groups symmetries as
n\times n
matrices, which significantly simplifies the computations performed.

Although unapologetically restrictive compared to the general problem of determining arbitrary actions of arbitrary groups on arbitrary data sets, the constrained setting of representations of compact Lie groups in point clouds still sets the stage for many interesting problems in data science, some of which we motivate in the examples below, further explored in Section
6
.

Example 1.1

(Pixel permutations, Section
6.1
)
.

Consider a set of
m_{x}\times m_{y}
-pixeled images such that each of them is generated by a simple permutation of the pixels of another fixed image. Supposing these permutations form a finite group
\Sigma
, then the embedding of the images in
\mathbb{R}^{m_{x}\times m_{y}}
will necessarily lie on a representation orbit of
\Sigma
in
\mathbb{R}^{m_{x}\times m_{y}}
.
As it turns out, when
\Sigma
is Abelian, the data also lies on an orbit of the compact Lie group
T^{d}
, the
d
-dimensional torus, which may then be used to reconstruct the group from the embedded vectors only.

Example 1.2

(Harmonic analysis, Section
6.2
)
.

Abstract harmonic analysis is often referred to as the study of signal decomposition in arbitrary groups. For the case of compact Lie groups, the existence of a discrete orthonormal basis for the set of
L^{2}
-integrable functions is guaranteed by the Peter-Weyl theorem, which implies that such a basis is parametrized by
\widehat{G}
, the complex irreducible representations of
G
. In particular, ordinary harmonic analysis, taken as the decomposition of periodic functions through sines and cosines, is but a special case of this result.
Suppose then
\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{N}
is a data set, where each
x_{i}
lies in the orbit
\mathcal{O}
of a representation of a compact Lie group
G
,
y_{i}
are real scalars, and there is an unknown function
f:\mathcal{O}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
f(x_{i})\approx y_{i}
. If
\{f_{[\phi]}\}_{[\phi]\in\hat{G}}
is an abstract harmonic analysis basis for
G
, then this regression can be written as

f=\sum_{[\phi]\in\widehat{G}}a_{[\phi]}f_{[\phi]},

where
a_{[\phi]}
are complex constants. Not only does the summation above converge in the whole function’s domain, but the functions
f_{[\phi]}
can be known for every choice of
G
. Therefore, the knowledge that the input data lies close to an orbit of a compact Lie group
G
suggests a linear regression solution to the machine learning problem.

Example 1.3

(Hamiltonian systems, Section
6.3
)
.

The fundamental role of Lie groups in modern Physics, especially in its more mathematical formulation, was initially identified by the Noether theorem, which established the well-known one-to-one correspondence between actions of Lie groups and conserved variables in classical mechanics
[
69
]
. The following decades, however, not only expanded this correspondence to many other areas of Physics—from general relativity to quantum field theory—but was used for solving several classical problems
[
91
]
. Due to its more geometric formulation, Hamiltonian mechanics is particularly susceptible to receiving treatment through Lie theory in determining conserved observables, allowing for the direct derivation of constants of motion from the knowledge of Lie action orbits.

Our contribution and related work.

In Algorithm
3.1
, we propose a framework to transform the problems from the last examples in machine learning tasks. The precise model assumed will be formalized in Section
3
, but simply put, we take a representation
\phi:G\to\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
of a compact Lie group
G
in some vector space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, for which
\mathcal{O}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is one of its orbits. We want to determine
\phi
using only a point cloud
X
well enough sampled in
\mathcal{O}
. Moreover, because the knowledge of
\phi
permits the reconstruction
\mathcal{O}
, it is possible to determine which of the compact Lie groups is most likely to have generated the orbit. Unfortunately, this is an ill-posed problem: two distinct compact Lie groups can generate the same orbit
\mathcal{O}
; moreover, fixed a compact Lie group
G
, two of its distinct representations can also generate
\mathcal{O}
. Ignoring these subtleties for now, we stress that, as far as we know, ours is the first reported technique in literature to identify both the precise representation type and the most likely acting Lie group. We believe that such information may be fruitful in developing new inference techniques and application settings, some of which we already suggest here. Besides, the theoretical guarantees of Section
5
already imply strong robustness results to our algorithm, which is seen to work even when available data
X
does not sit perfectly within
\mathcal{O}
.

On the other hand, although new in its precise structure, our approach is not the first attempt to solve this sort of problem, given the long history of interest in action detection problems. For example, as indicated by
[
78
]
, the related problem of symmetry identification has been tackled since the 1980s, with algorithms for symmetries of
\text{SE}(2)
representations in planar data
[
92
,
44
]
. A similar task, now for 3D objects, was often addressed in the literature, as well-reviewed by
[
66
]
. We stress, however, that all these works are limited to the detection of symmetries as subgroups of known groups, a scope much more restricted than ours.

The above limitation was addressed by some research aimed at automatically detecting the representation operators of more general Lie groups from different data sets. Sohl-Dickstein, Wand, and Olshausen, for example, use a modified version of the MAP algorithm to estimate the operators
T
such that
\sum_{t}|x^{(t)}-Tx^{(t-1)}|
is minimized
[
80
]
. Although having the obvious advantage of dealing with naturally sequential data—the paper consider applications of this method to videos, for example—the technique is not general enough to consider arbitrary commutation of group elements, being perhaps better suited to Abelian Lie groups. Cohen and Welling, once more, also address the problem of determining the exact representation operators from the orbit points using a Bayesian approach; however, besides being restricted only to Abelian Lie groups, they do not try to project the operators into the closest Lie algebra, meaning that no information about the exact representation type is retrieved
[
20
]
. A more recent approach, now useful even for orbits of non-Abelian Lie groups, was introduced by
[
73
]
in response to the problem raised by
[
43
]
which, using methods from Riemannian geometry, estimates local basis for orbits of direct products of Lie groups.

In
[
26
]
, on the other hand, the authors use a generalization of the
G
-CNN architecture, now to Lie algebras convolutions, to estimate derived representation operators, denoted
X
, in data sets for supervised tasks that are equivariant under some unknown Lie group
G
. Although similar to our work, this approach not only again gives the Lie algebra operators only as a vector space—i.e., there is no attempt to project the estimated
X
onto the closest derived representation of
G
—but the output may also be an over-determined set of vector, meaning that it does not even form a basis for the Lie algebra of
G
. This issue is resolved by Cahill
et al.
in
[
15
]
, where the exact Lie algebra of the symmetry group of the orbit, denoted by
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
, seen only as a vector space, is estimated, together with its dimension, through spectral decomposition of an operator. Our algorithm is, in fact, a generalization of this last work, where instead of only finding the basis for
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
we project it into the closest representation of the associated Lie group. Moreover, while the approach by Cahill
et al.
permits an estimation of the symmetry group only, our generalization allows for estimating the representation of different Lie groups which also have actions in the embedding space of orbit
\mathcal{O}
.

Once the information of acting Lie groups is determined, it can be used to increase the efficiency of other inference and machine learning tasks. In this context, the interest often lies in devising equivariant algorithms, that is, maps
\Phi:X\to Y
from the input to the output spaces such that, for a fixed group
G
with actions on
\cdot_{X}
and
\cdot_{Y}
in
X
and
Y
, we have
\Phi(g\cdot_{X}X)=g\cdot_{Y}\Phi(X)
for all
g\in G
. In particular, already in 1995, LeCun
et al.
showed that convolution neural networks
[
58
]
, introduced by some of the same authors in 1989, have all their convolution and pooling layers equivariant under translations in the pixel grid
[
57
]
. Nevertheless, it was only in 2016 that Cohen and Welling extended this architecture to general group actions
[
21
]
, achieving the state-of-the-art at the time for the rotated MNIST data set
[
56
]
through employing finite groups. These equivariant networks for pixel data were later expanded to Lie group actions in works such as
[
94
,
55
,
27
,
18
,
8
,
32
,
93
,
90
]
, where equivariance under the usual
\text{SE}(2)=\mathrm{SO}(2)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
representation in the plane is guaranteed using different techniques and then applied to problems ranging from microscopic imaging to galaxy morphology classification. Solutions to pixel data in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, here equivariant under the natural
\mathrm{SO}(3)
representations, were developed
[
22
]
.
Similar
G
-CNN architectures were suggested for problems equivariant under representations of
\text{SE}(3)

[
38
,
37
,
83
]
to dynamical systems conservation laws
[
32
]
, and the Lorentz group, responsible for describing relativistic particles behavior
[
11
]
. Finally, Finzi, Welling, and Wilson suggested a general automatic technique for achieving equivariant architectures for any Lie group representation
[
33
]
.
In contrast, Kondor and Trivedi showed that, under minor assumptions,
G
-CNN implementations are the only feed-forward neural network architectures equivariant for compact Lie group representations
[
54
]
.

Technical aspects.

In addition to applications, this article places particular emphasis on theoretical justification.
As far as geometry is concerned, we follow standard ideas of the literature regarding manifold and tangent space estimation, involving notions of geometric measure theory
[
1
,
12
]
.
In particular, we adopt a measure-theoretic point of view of the problem.
This is part of a movement in recent research to express stability results with the Wasserstein distance, instead of the traditional Hausdorff distance, less suited to the case of noisy data sets
[
14
,
60
,
85
]
.
On the algebraic side, at the core of our algorithm lies Lie-PCA, a recent method for estimating the Lie algebra of symmetry groups
[
15
]
.
We analyze it by invoking traditional tools of representation theory.
Last, regarding implementations, we raise concrete matrix computation problems, such as simultaneous reduction
[
76
,
89
,
70
,
63
]
and local PCA
[
88
,
52
,
1
,
79
,
15
]
.
Throughout this paper, and although we do not enter into further details, we draw connections with problems of other areas of mathematics, such as varieties of Lie algebras
[
36
,
74
,
62
,
6
,
3
,
86
]
, Linnik type problems
[
61
,
29
,
28
,
75
,
49
]
, and rigidity of Lie subalgebras
[
5
,
4
,
23
,
7
]
.

Code availability.

We developed a Python library,
LieDetect
, which fully implements the algorithm described in this paper. This can be found at
https://github.com/HLovisiEnnes/LieDetect
, together with our experiments from Section
6
.
In addition, some video animations, illustrating the minimization program performed during the algorithm, are gathered at
https://www.youtube.com/playlist?list=PL_FkltNTtklBQlwrGyAnisJ-lGiLFeTVw
.

Outline.

The rest of the paper is as follows.
In Section
2
, we give a self-contained introduction to the theory of representations of Lie groups.
We define the main algorithm of this paper in Section
3
and give additional comments regarding its implementation in Section
4
.
We prove consistency and stability results for the algorithm in Section
5
.
In Section
6
we apply the algorithm to three concrete machine learning problems.
Notations are listed in Appendix
A.1
, and a few supplementary results are gathered in Appendix
A.2
.

2
Preliminaries

We now lay the theoretical background needed to read this paper.
The first two sections introduce basic notions regarding Lie groups and their representations, following
[
13
]
.
The third section is dedicated to defining and analyzing the structure of the Stiefel and Grassmann varieties of Lie algebras, which will play a crucial role in our algorithm.

2.1
Lie groups and Lie algebras

2.1.1 Lie groups

A
Lie group
is a group
G
that is also a smooth manifold, and such that the multiplication map
(g,h)\mapsto gh
and the inverse map
g\mapsto g^{-1}
are smooth.
An example is given by the general linear group
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
of real invertible
n\times n
matrices, and its complex equivalent
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
.
Endowed with the subspace topology inherited from the vector space of
n\times n
matrices
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, both
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
are smooth submanifolds, and also Lie groups.
They have real dimensions
n^{2}
and
2n^{2}
, respectively, and are not compact.
In what follows, we will focus on compact Lie groups, and more particularly on the orthogonal group
\mathrm{O}(n)
, the special orthogonal group
\mathrm{SO}(n)
, the unitary group
\mathrm{U}(n)
, the special unitary group
\mathrm{SU}(n)
, and the torus
T^{n}
, respectively defined for any integer
n\geq 1
as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{O}(n)

\displaystyle=\big\{A\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\mid A^{\top}=A^{-1}\big\},

\displaystyle\mathrm{SO}(n)

\displaystyle=\big\{A\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\mid A^{\top}=A^{-1},\det(A)=1\big\},

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}(n)

\displaystyle=\big\{A\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})\mid A^{*}=A^{-1}\big\},

\displaystyle\mathrm{SU}(n)

\displaystyle=\big\{A\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})\mid A^{*}=A^{-1},\det(A)=1\big\},

\displaystyle T^{n}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{SO}(2)\times\cdots\times\mathrm{SO}(2)~~~~\text{(}n\text{ copies)},

where
A^{\top}
denote the transpose and
A^{*}
the conjugate transpose.
These groups have respective dimensions
n(n-1)/2
,
n(n-1)/2
,
n^{2}
,
n^{2}-1
and
n
, and are all connected, expect for
\mathrm{O}(n)
which consists of two connected components.
Moreover, they are all
matrix groups
, that is, they can be described as subgroups of a general linear group
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
or
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
.
In general, it is a consequence of Peter–Weyl theorem that any compact Lie group is a matrix group.

A
Lie group homomorphism
is a smooth group homomorphism between two Lie groups. For instance, embedding
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
into
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
and
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
into
\mathrm{M}_{2n}(\mathbb{R})
yield Lie group homomorphisms
\mathrm{SO}(n)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{SU}(n)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{SO}(2n)
.
If a Lie group homomorphism is bijective with smooth inverse, it is called a
Lie group isomorphism
, and the Lie groups are said isomorphic.
In the list above, all the groups are non-isomorphic, except for
\mathrm{SO}(1)\simeq\mathrm{SU}(1)
, both singletons, and
\mathrm{SO}(2)\simeq\mathrm{U}(1)\simeq T^{1}
.
Moreover, any Abelian Lie group of dimension
d
is isomorphic to
T^{d}
.

It is worth mentioning that being a Lie group imposes strong regularities on the underlying manifold.
For instance, they are all orientable.
Moreover, the fundamental group of a connected Lie group is discrete and Abelian.
As another example, the
n
-sphere
S^{n}
admits a Lie group structure only when
n=0
,
1
or
3
, these cases corresponding respectively to the singleton,
\mathrm{SO}(2)
and
\mathrm{SU}(2)
.
Besides, being a Lie group implies the existence of a Lie algebra structure on the tangent spaces, as we review now.

2.1.2 Lie algebras

A
Lie algebra
is a vector space
V
, together with an operation
[\cdot,\cdot]\colon V\times V\to V
, called
Lie bracket
, that is bilinear, anti-symmetric, and obeys Jacobi’s identity, that is,
[x,[y,z]]+[y,[z,x]]+[z,[x,y]]=0
for all
x,y,z\in V
.
If
[A,B]=0
for all
A,B\in V
, then the Lie algebra is said
Abelian
.
By fixing a basis
\{A_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
of the vector space
V
, the
structure constants
of the Lie algebra relative to this basis are defined as the scalars
\{c_{i,j}^{k}\}_{i,j,k=1}^{m}
satisfying
[A_{i},A_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{m}c_{i,j}^{k}A_{k}
for all
i,j\in[1\dots m]
.
As a particular case, the structure constants of an Abelian Lie algebra are all zero and hence do not depend on the basis.
As another example, one defines a Lie bracket on the space of
n\times n
matrices
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
via the
commutator

[A,B]=AB-BA
.
This Lie algebra is non-Abelian when
n\geq 2
.

Given a Lie group
G
, its tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{e}G
at the origin can be canonically endowed with a Lie bracket, yielding a Lie algebra, denoted
\mathfrak{g}
.
This construction is commonly based on left-invariant vector fields on
G
, which we do not describe here.
When
G\subset\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
is a matrix group, a more direct definition exists: the tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{e}G
, seen as a linear subspace of
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, is endowed with the Lie bracket of commutator of matrices.
By convention, we write in gothic font the Lie algebra.
For instance, the tangent spaces of the general linear group, the special orthogonal group, the special unitary group, and the torus are

\displaystyle\mathfrak{gl}(n)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{so}(n)

\displaystyle=\{A\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\mid A^{\top}=-A\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{su}(n)

\displaystyle=\{A\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})\mid A^{*}=-A,~\mathrm{tr}(A)=0\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}^{n}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{so}(2)\times\cdots\times\mathfrak{so}(2)~~~\text{(}n\text{ copies)},

i.e.,
\mathfrak{so}(n)
is the set of skew-symmetric matrices and
\mathfrak{su}(n)
of zero-trace skew-Hermitian matrices.

A
Lie algebra homomorphism
between two Lie algebras
V
and
W
is a linear map
\phi\colon V\rightarrow W
such that
[\phi(A),\phi(B)]=\phi([A,B])
for all
A,B\in V
. If
\phi
is bijective, it is called an
isomorphism of Lie algebras
, and the Lie algebras are said isomorphic.
Equivalently, two Lie algebras are isomorphic if they admit the same structure constants in a certain pair of bases.
For instance, as shown in the example below,
\mathfrak{su}(2)
and
\mathfrak{so}(3)
are isomorphic, although
\mathrm{SU}(2)
and
\mathrm{SO}(3)
are not.
This shows that the Lie algebra does not totally determine a Lie group.
Despite that fact, it contains valuable information about the group, as we explain in the next section.

Example 2.1
.

A usual choice of basis for
\mathfrak{so}(3)
, as presented in
[
30
]
, is

X_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&-1\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix},~~X_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
0&0&0\\
-1&0&0\end{pmatrix},~~X_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix},

for which we have the brackets
[X_{1},X_{2}]=X_{3}
,
[X_{2},X_{3}]=X_{1}
and
[X_{1},X_{3}]=-X_{2}
.
Besides, a basis of
\mathfrak{su}(2)
is given by the Pauli matrices, which yield the same Lie brackets:

Y_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix},~~Y_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},~~Y_{3}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}i&0\\
0&-i\end{pmatrix}.

Example 2.2
.

It is known that any matrix in
\mathrm{SO}(2)
can be written as
R(\theta)
for some
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
, and any matrix in
\mathfrak{so}(2)
as
L(a)
for some
a\in\mathbb{R}
, where

R(\theta)=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\
\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}~~~~~\mathrm{and}~~~~~L(a)=\begin{pmatrix}0&-a\\
a&0\end{pmatrix}.

Consequently, matrices of
T^{d}
can be written as
\mathrm{diag}(R(\theta_{1}),\dots,R(\theta_{d}))
for some
(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{d})\in[0,2\pi)^{d}
, and matrices of
\mathfrak{t}^{d}
as
\mathrm{diag}(L(a_{1}),\dots,L(a_{d}))
for some
(a_{1},\dots,a_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

2.1.3 The exponential map

Given a Lie group
G
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, there exists a canonical smooth map
\exp\colon\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow G
, called the
exponential map
.
In the general construction, it is defined using one-parameter subgroups generated by left-invariant vector fields.
In the particular case of matrix groups
G\subset\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, the exponential map is simply the restriction to
\mathfrak{g}
of the matrix exponential,
\exp\colon\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, defined as
\exp(A)=\sum_{i=0}^{+\infty}A^{n}/n!
.
For
A,B\in\mathfrak{g}
, it holds

\exp(A+B)=\exp(A)\exp(B)\iff[A,B]=0.

Endowing
\mathfrak{g}
with the group structure of the addition, we deduce that
\exp\colon\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow G
is a group homomorphism only when
\mathfrak{g}
is an Abelian Lie algebra, that is, when
G
is a torus, in the compact case.
Moreover, if
G
is connected and compact or is the general linear group, then the exponential map is subjective.
For instance, elements of
\mathrm{SO}(2)
take the form
R(\theta)
for some
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, which in turn is equal to the exponential of
L(\theta)
, defined in Example
2.2
.

The exponential map is a fundamental tool in the study of Lie groups, allowing a dialogue between the group and its algebra.
To illustrate this phenomenon, any Lie group homomorphism
\phi\colon G\to H
induces a unique Lie algebra homomorphism
\mathrm{d}\phi\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{h}
with commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{d}\phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp}

(1)

The map
\mathrm{d}\phi
is called the
derived homomorphism
of
\phi
, and
\mathrm{d}\phi(\mathfrak{g})\subset\mathfrak{h}
the
pushforward Lie algebra
.
If
\phi
is an isomorphism, or surjective with discrete kernel, then
\mathrm{d}\phi
is an isomorphism.
For instance, the well-known double cover
\mathrm{SU}(2)\rightarrow\mathrm{SO}(3)
yields an isomorphism
\mathfrak{su}(2)\rightarrow\mathfrak{so}(3)
.

In Riemannian geometry, there is a concurrent notion of exponential map.
Without entering into the details, we mention that any compact Lie group admits a bi-invariant Riemannian metric. In this case, the Riemannian exponential coincides with the (Lie) exponential
[
65
]
.
On simple Lie groups, such as
\mathrm{SO}(n)
with
n\neq 4
, such a metric is unique up to a constant.

The Frobenius inner product
\langle A,B\rangle=\Tr(AB^{\top})
on the Lie algebra
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
defines a metric on
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
.
In the rest of this paper, if
G
is
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
or one of its subgroups, it will be endowed with this metric, making it a Riemannian manifold.
This metric is not bi-invariant on
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, however, it is when restricted to
\mathrm{SO}(n)
.
On
\mathrm{SO}(3)
, the corresponding geodesic distance is equal to
\mathrm{d}(P,Q)=\arccos((\langle P,Q\rangle-1)/2)
, which is also the absolute value of the angle of the rotation
PQ^{\top}

[
67
]
.
More generally, the geodesic distance on
\mathrm{SO}(n)
is explicitly given by the quadratic mean of its angles.
Note that
\mathrm{SO}(n)
admits other non-equivalent bi-invariant
distances
(not metrics), such as that induced by the Frobenius norm, called in this context the chordal distance.

On a compact Lie group, there exists a unique left-invariant probability measure
\mu_{G}
, i.e., such that
\mu_{G}(gA)=\mu_{G}(A)
for any
g\in G
and any Borel subset
A\subset G
. It is called the
Haar measure
.
If
G
is endowed with a bi-invariant Riemannian structure, then the Haar measure is equal to the Riemannian volume form.
This notion proves to be an invaluable tool, first for being a canonical probability measure on
G
, and second for enabling the technique of ‘averaging’ on Lie group.
In particular, we will use the Haar measure to ‘orthogonalize’ representations in Section
2.2.1
, and to define the uniform measure on the orbits in Section
2.2.3
.

2.1.4 Intrinsic and extrinsic symmetries

Lie groups naturally appear when studying the symmetries of an object.
To exemplify this statement, let us consider a Riemannian manifold
\mathcal{M}
and its
isometry group

\mathrm{Isom}(\mathcal{M})
, defined as the set of diffeomorphisms
\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
that preserves the metric.
The Myers–Steenrod theorem states that
\mathrm{Isom}(\mathcal{M})
, when given the topology of pointwise convergence, forms a Lie group
[
68
]
. Moreover, it is compact when
\mathcal{M}
is.
For instance, the
n
-sphere endowed with the metric inherited from
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
satisfies
\mathrm{Isom}(S^{n})\simeq\mathrm{O}(n+1)
.
For the flat torus
S^{1}\times S^{1}
in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, it is a semi-direct product
\mathrm{Isom}(S^{1}\times S^{1})\simeq D_{4}\rtimes\mathrm{SO}(2)\times\mathrm{SO}(2)
, where
D_{4}
is the dihedral group.
As a last example, the isometry group of
\mathrm{SO}(3)
, endowed with its bi-invariant Riemannian structure, takes the form
\mathrm{SO}(3)\rtimes\mathrm{SO}(3)\times\{\pm 1\}

[
39
]
.

If
\mathcal{M}
is isometrically embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, a closely related concept is that of
symmetry group
, defined as
\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{M})=\{P\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\mid P\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}\}
.
It can be understood as the set of ‘extrinsic isometries’ of
\mathcal{M}
.
It is a closed subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, hence a Lie group by Cartan’s theorem
[
17
]
.
By restricting the action of the matrices
P
to
\mathcal{M}
, we obtain a group homomorphism

\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}(\mathcal{M}).

It may not be injective, since certain matrices
P
may act trivially on
\mathcal{M}
.
For instance, if
\mathcal{M}
is embedded on the first
n
coordinates of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
, then any transformation
P\in\mathrm{O}(n+m)
that stabilizes
\mathbb{R}^{n}
will be an element of
\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{M})
, although being trivial when seen in
\mathrm{Isom}(\mathcal{M})
.

In this paper, it will be convenient to work with the Lie algebra of
\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{M})
, denoted
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{M})
.
It consists of the matrices of
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
that exponentiate to an element of
\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{M})
.
In
[
15
]
is given the following convenient formulation, for which we give a proof in Section
A.2.1
.

\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{M})=\big\{A\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\mid\forall x\in\mathcal{M},Ax\in\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}\big\}.

(2)

In what follows, we will frequently refer to the following hypothesis: all the points of
\mathcal{M}
are at equal distance from the origin, and its linear span
\langle\mathcal{M}\rangle
is equal to the whole ambient space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
In this case, a matrix
P\in\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{M})
must preserve the Euclidean norm, hence is an orthogonal matrix.
That is,
\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{M})\subset\mathrm{O}(n)
, and consequently
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{M})\subset\mathfrak{so}(n)
.

2.2
Representations of Lie groups

2.2.1 Definitions

A (real)
representation
of a Lie group
G
in a (real) vector space
V
is a Lie group homomorphism
G\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}(V)
.
When
V
is a space of dimension
n
, the representation is said
n
-dimensional.
Similarly, a
representation
of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
in
V
is a Lie algebra homomorphism
\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}(V)
.
Let us suppose that
V=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and consider a representation of
G
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, denoted
\phi\colon G\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
.
As we have seen in Equation (
1
), the derived homomorphism
\mathrm{d}\phi
is a representation of
\mathfrak{g}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, fitting in the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{d}\phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp}

(3)

That is to say, there is a map

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\colon\mathrm{Rep}(G,\mathbb{R}^{n})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{Rep}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{R}^{n})

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathrm{d}\phi

between the set of representations of
G
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and the set of representations of
\mathfrak{g}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
When the exponential map of
G
is surjective, for instance when
G
is connected and compact, then the diagram above shows that the map
\mathrm{d}
is injective.
Moreover, when
G
is simply connected, then
\mathrm{d}
is also surjective, a phenomenon known as the Lie group–Lie algebra correspondence.
However, among the groups of interest in this paper, only
\mathrm{SU}(n)
is simply connected.

A representation of a Lie group
G
(resp. of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
) is said
faithful
if the homomorphism
G\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
(resp.
\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
) is injective, and
almost-faithful
if its kernel is discrete.
Clearly, a representation
G\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
is almost-faithful if and only if the derived representation
\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
is faithful.
In this case, the pushforward algebra
\mathrm{d}\phi(\mathfrak{g})
is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{g}
via
\mathrm{d}\phi
.

Two representations
\phi
and
\psi
of a same group
G
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
are said
equivalent
if there exists
M\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\psi(g)=M\phi(g)M^{-1}
for all
g\in G
.
This invites us to, given a representation, look for the ‘simplest’ representation equivalent to it.
To this end, we say that a representation is
orthogonal
if it takes values in
\mathrm{O}(n)
.
As it turns out, when
G
is compact, any representation is equivalent to an orthogonal one
[
13
, Th. I.7]
.
Moreover, if two orthogonal representations are equivalent, then they are also orthogonally equivalent, meaning that the matrix
M\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
can be chosen in
\mathrm{O}(n)
.
We will refer to this process as ‘orthogonalization’ of representations.
As we will explain in Section
5.1.2
, this result is proved via averaging with the Haar measure.

Example 2.3
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the application
\phi_{k}\colon\mathrm{SO}(2)\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
defined below is a representation of
\mathrm{SO}(2)
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, whose derived representation
\mathrm{d}\phi_{k}\colon\mathfrak{so}(2)\rightarrow\mathrm{M}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
is denoted
L_{k}
:

\phi_{k}(\theta)=\begin{pmatrix}\cos k\theta&-\sin k\theta\\
\sin k\theta&\cos k\theta\end{pmatrix}~~~~~~~~\mathrm{and}~~~~~~~~L_{k}(\theta)=\begin{pmatrix}0&-k\theta\\
k\theta&0\end{pmatrix}.

Besides, the representation

\theta\mapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\theta&0\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)

of
\mathfrak{so}(2)
does not come from a representation of
\mathrm{SO}(2)
.

Example 2.4
.

For
\alpha>1
, the following representation
\phi
of
\mathrm{SO}(2)
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is not orthogonal, but
M\phi M^{-1}
is equal to the orthogonal representation
\phi_{1}
defined in Example
2.3
, where

\phi(\theta)=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\alpha\sin\theta\\
\alpha^{-1}\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}~~~~~~\mathrm{and}~~~~~~~~M=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&\alpha\end{pmatrix}.~~~~~~~~~

2.2.2 Irreducible representations

Let
\phi
be a representation of a Lie group
G
in some vector space
V
.
It is called
reducible
if there exists a proper linear subspace
W\subset V
stabilized by
\phi
, that is, such that
\phi(g)W\subset W
for all
g\in G
. Otherwise,
\phi
is said
irreducible
, and is called an
irrep
.
Any orthogonal representation
\phi\colon G\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is
completely decomposable
, meaning that there exists an integer
p
, a decomposition
\mathbb{R}^{n}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{p}W_{i}
into
p
pairwise orthogonal subspaces, and a collection of irreducible representations
\{\phi_{i}\colon G\rightarrow W_{i}\}_{i=1}^{p}
such that
\phi
is isomorphic to the direct sum
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{p}\phi_{i}
.
In other words, by denoting
O\in\mathrm{O}(n)
the change of basis into the decomposition given by the
W_{i}
’s, the representation
\phi
can be written, in matrix notation, as

\phi\colon g\mapsto O\mathrm{diag}\big(\phi_{1}(g),\dots,\phi_{p}(g)\big)O^{\top}.

This integer
p
is uniquely defined, as well as the irreps, up to permutation.

This result shows that one is able to obtain any representation of
G
by simply summing irreducible representations.
Having access to a list of these representations is thus a crucial problem to work with Lie groups.
Although real irreducible representations of simple real Lie algebras are fully classified for a long time
[
51
]
, it does not mean the representative matrices are explicitly known.
We give below this list of irreps for the groups of interest in this paper.

Example 2.5
.

It is known that the irreducible representations of
\mathrm{SO}(2)
consist of the trivial representation
\mathrm{SO}(2)\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}(1)
, denoted
\phi_{0}
, as well as the infinite family
\{\phi_{k}\mid k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}\}
, where
\phi_{k}\colon\theta\mapsto R(k\theta)
, and
R
is the matrix defined in Example
2.2
.

Example 2.6
.

As a generalization of the
\mathrm{SO}(2)
case, the irreducible representations of the torus
T^{d}
, apart from the trivial representation, form an infinite family of representations of dimension two, denoted
\phi_{\lambda}
, and depending on a parameter
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, called the
weight
, where
\phi_{\lambda}(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{d})=R\big(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}\theta_{i}\big)
, and
R
is defined in Example
2.2
.

Example 2.7
.

Because
\mathrm{SU}(2)
is the double-cover of
\mathrm{SO}(3)
, all representations of the latter yield representations of the former.
Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, both groups admit at most one irrep into
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, up to equivalence.
Moreover,
\mathrm{SO}(3)
admits an irrep when
n
is odd, and
\mathrm{SU}(2)
admits an irrep when
n
is odd or
n\equiv 0\pmod{4}
.
Explicit formulas are given in Appendix
A.2.2
.

2.2.3 Structure of the orbits

Consider a representation
\phi\colon G\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
and a point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
The main object of study of this paper is the
orbit
of
x
generated by
G
, defined as the set
\mathcal{O}=\{\phi(g)x\mid g\in G\}
.
In what follows, we will suppose that
x
is not fixed by the action, otherwise the orbit would be trivial.
Most of what can be said about orbits of representation of Lie groups also holds in the more general setting of orbits of actions of Lie groups.
Among their properties, we cite that orbits are submanifolds of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, they are homogeneous
G
-spaces, and they satisfy the orbit-stabilizer theorem
[
59
]
.
Besides, the map
\phi
induces a homomorphism
G\rightarrow\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{O})
.
At the level of Lie algebras, and as in Equation (
3
), we deduce that

\mathrm{d}\phi(\mathfrak{g})\subset\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O}).

This inclusion will have great importance, since it indicates that
G
can be ‘found’ in
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
.

As an example, the group
\mathrm{SO}(n)
acts on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by left-multiplication, and its orbits are
(n-1)
-spheres.
Similarly,
\mathrm{SU}(n)
acts on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
by left-multiplication, generating
(2n-1)
-spheres.
Besides, the torus
T^{d}=\mathrm{SO}(2)\times\cdots\times\mathrm{SO}(2)
acts on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and the orbit of a point
x
is a
d
-torus, provided that
x
is not zero in one of the planes
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
It is worth mentioning that other representations yield a variety of orbits.
For instance,
\mathrm{SO}(n)
acts on
\mathrm{M}_{n,d}(\mathbb{R})
, the space of
n\times d
matrices, by simultaneous left-multiplication of the
d
columns.
If
x\in\mathrm{M}_{n,d}(\mathbb{R})
has rank
k
, then its orbit is homeomorphic to
\mathcal{V}(k,\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the Stiefel manifold of
k
-frames in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

We remind the reader that a compact Lie group admits a canonical probability measure
\mu_{G}
, called the Haar measure (see Section
2.1.3
).
In this paper, orbits
\mathcal{O}
will always be endowed with their
uniform measure
, denoted
\mu_{\mathcal{O}}
, and defined as the pushforward of
\mu_{G}
via the map
g\mapsto\phi(g)x
, which does not depend on
x\in\mathcal{O}
.
One shows the following equivalent definition: with
l
the dimension of
\mathcal{O}
, it holds that
\mu_{\mathcal{O}}
admits a constant density over the
l
-dimensional Haudorff measure
\mathcal{H}^{l}
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
restricted to
\mathcal{O}
, this constant being equal to
\mathcal{H}^{l}(\mathcal{O})^{-1}
, the inverse of its volume.

As formalized in Section
3
, the aim of this paper is to recover, given an orbit
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the representation
\phi\colon G\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
that generates it.
This problem is ill-posed, since several representations may generate
\mathcal{O}
.
With this limitation in mind, our method will aim at recovering any one of those.
In order to make this issue more rigorous, we say that two representations
\phi,\phi^{\prime}\colon G\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
are
orbit-equivalent
if there exists a matrix
M\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
such that the pushforward Lie algebras
\mathrm{d}\phi(\mathfrak{g})
and
M\mathrm{d}\phi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{g})M^{-1}
, seen as linear subspaces of
\mathfrak{so}(n)
, are equal.
In particular, their orbits are conjugated.
Note that when the representations are orthogonal, the matrix
M
can be chosen in
\mathrm{O}(n)
.
We denote by
\mathrm{OrbRep}(G,n)
the collection of orbit-equivalence classes of representations of
G
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Clearly, equivalent representations are orbit-equivalent, but the converse is not true.
For instance, all the non-trivial irreps of
\mathrm{SO}(2)
are orbit-equivalent.
More generally, for any surjective homomorphism
f\colon G\rightarrow G
, the composition
f\circ\phi
is orbit-equivalent to
\phi
.
We will study the cases
\mathrm{SO}(2)
,
T^{d}
,
\mathrm{SO}(3)
and
\mathrm{SU}(2)
in Section
4
.

2.2.4 Abstract harmonic analysis

It is a generalization of classical Fourier analysis to signals over arbitrary groups, and has been used in recent years in the context of data analysis, as reviewed by
[
19
]
.
In opposition with the rest of this paper, which only works with real representations, we will consider in this paragraph, and in Section
6.2
,
complex
representations.
In this context, we define a
matrix coefficient
as a function
f:G\to\mathbb{C}
of form

f_{u,v}^{V}(g)=\langle g\cdot u,v\rangle,

where
V
is a complex unitary representation of
G
, and
u,v
any vectors in
V
. In particular, if
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
is a basis for
V
, then the matrix coefficient
f^{V}_{e_{i},e_{j}}(g)
is equal to
\phi_{ij}(g)
, the
ij^{\mathrm{th}}
coordinate of the matrix
\phi(g)
, hence the name.
The core foundation of abstract harmonic analysis is established by the famous Peter-Weyl theorem, which allows for decomposing signals over compact Lie groups—or their orbits—using only a subset of matrix coefficients
[
35
,
72
]
.

In order to state the theorem, let us denote by
\widehat{G}
the set of unitary (complex) irreducible representations of a Lie group
G
, up to equivalence.
To an irrep
[\phi]\in\widehat{G}
corresponds a vector space
V_{\phi}
.
The theorem states that
\widehat{G}
is countable, and that
L^{2}(G)
, the set of square-integrable functions on
G\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, has an orthonormal basis given by

\big\{\sqrt{\dim V_{\phi}}\cdot\phi_{ij}\mid[\phi]\in\widehat{G},~i,j\in[1\dots\dim V_{\phi}]\big\}.

In particular, if
f\in L^{2}(G)
, then we can decompose

f(g)=\sum_{[\phi]\in\widehat{G}}\dim V_{\phi}\cdot\Tr\big(\widehat{f}(\phi)\pi(g)\big)~~~~~\mathrm{where}~~~~~\widehat{f}(\phi)_{ij}=\int_{G}f(g)\phi_{ij}^{\dagger}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}(g),

(4)

in which we take the dagger to represent the complex conjugate of the matrix, and we compute the integral over the Haar measure.
Although we do not discuss them here, several important results from ordinary harmonic analysis, such as the Parseval formula, the unitarity of the transform
f\mapsto\hat{f}
, and the convolution theorem can also be shown in this more general setting.

Example 2.8
.

It is a well-known fact that the complex irreps of
\mathrm{SO}(2)
have dimension
1
and are given by
\widehat{\mathrm{SO}}(2)=\big\{e^{ni\theta}\mid\theta\in[0,2\pi),n\in\mathbb{Z}\big\}
. Therefore, applying Equation (
4
) yields

f(\theta)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{in\theta}\widehat{f}(n)~~~~~\mathrm{where}~~~~~\widehat{f}(n)=\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{-in\theta}f(\theta)\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi},

which is exactly the usual Fourier decomposition of signals on the circle.
This can be easily generalized to the torus
T^{d}
, for which
\widehat{T^{d}}=\big\{e^{i(n_{1}\theta_{1}+\dots+n_{d}\theta_{d})}\mid n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},\theta\in[0,2\pi)^{d}\big\}
, yielding to

\begin{split}f(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{d})&=\sum_{n_{1},\dots,n_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{i(n_{1}\theta_{1}+\dots+n_{d}\theta_{d})}\widehat{f}(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})\\
\mathrm{where}~~~~~\widehat{f}(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})&=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{[0,2\pi)^{d}}e^{-i(n_{1}\theta_{1}+\dots+n_{d}\theta_{d})}f(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{d})\mathrm{d}\theta_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\theta_{d}.\end{split}

2.3
Moduli spaces of Lie algebras

2.3.1 Stiefel and Grassmann varieties of Lie algebras

In order to design an algorithm to work with Lie algebras, it will be convenient to understand the structure of the space they form.
First, we will embed them into a matrix space.
Let
n>0
be an integer,
\mathrm{SO}(n)
the special orthogonal group, and
\mathfrak{so}(n)
its Lie algebra, i.e., the set of
n\times n
skew-symmetric matrices.
Their dimension is
n(n-1)/2
.
We also endow
\mathfrak{so}(n)
with the Frobenius inner product
\langle A,B\rangle_{\mathrm{F}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{i,j}b_{i,j}
, with norm denoted
\|A\|_{\mathrm{F}}
.
Given an integer
d\geq 1
, we define

•

\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{Lie}}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))
, the
Grassmann variety of
d
-dimensional Lie subalgebras of
\mathfrak{so}(n)
, as the set of
d
-dimensional linear subspaces of
\mathfrak{so}(n)
that form a Lie algebra, i.e., are stable under Lie bracket.
It is embedded in
\mathrm{M}_{n(n-1)/2}(\mathbb{R})
, the
n(n-1)/2\times n(n-1)/2
matrices, by converting a subspace
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathfrak{so}(n)
into the orthogonal projection
\Pi\big[\mathcal{A}\big]\colon\mathfrak{so}(n)\rightarrow\mathfrak{so}(n)
on it.

•

\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{Lie}}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))
, the
Stiefel variety of
d
-dimensional Lie subalgebras of
\mathfrak{so}(n)
, as the set of orthonormal
d
-tuples of skew-symmetric matrices
(A_{1},\dots,A_{d})
whose linear span is a Lie algebra.
It is naturally a subset of
d
-fold product
\mathfrak{so}(n)^{d}
, hence also of
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})^{d}
.
We will also use another embedding, into
\mathrm{M}_{n^{2},d}(\mathbb{R})
, the
n^{2}\times d
matrices, obtained by converting a
d
-frame
(A_{1},\dots,A_{d})
onto the matrix whose
i^{\mathrm{th}}
column is the flattening of the matrix
A_{i}
.

It is worth mentioning that
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{Lie}}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))
and
\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{Lie}}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))
are subsets of the more general Grassmann and Stiefel manifolds of
d
-dimensional subspaces of
\mathfrak{so}(n)
, commonly denoted
\mathcal{G}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))
and
\mathcal{V}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))
, and where the condition of forming a Lie algebra is dropped.
These latter manifolds have dimension
(n(n-1)/2-d)d
and
(n(n-1)+d(d+1))d/2
respectively.

These spaces are linked by a surjective map
\pi\colon\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{Lie}}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))\rightarrow\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{Lie}}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))
, obtained by sending a
d
-frame
(A_{1},\dots,A_{d})
onto its span
\langle A_{1},\dots,A_{d}\rangle
.
Besides, two useful actions can be defined on them. First, by embedding
\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{Lie}}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))
as a subset of
\mathrm{M}_{n^{2},d}(\mathbb{R})
, we see that the orthogonal group
\mathrm{O}(d)
acts on it by right-multiplication, and we denote the action as
(A_{1},\dots,A_{d})\cdot O
.
It has the effect of rotating the frame into the space it spans.
Orbits of this action are exactly the fibers of
\pi
, allowing us to identify the quotient

{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.45613pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{Lie}}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathrm{O}(d)}$}}{\raisebox{4.20613pt}{$\textstyle{\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{Lie}}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\mathrm{O}(d)}$}}{\raisebox{3.2083pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{Lie}}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{O}(d)}$}}{\raisebox{2.42162pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{Lie}}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{O}(d)}$}}}\simeq\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{Lie}}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n)).

Next, seeing
\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{Lie}}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))
in
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})^{d}
, we have the action of
\mathrm{O}(n)
by simultaneous conjugation:

O\cdot(A_{1},\dots,A_{d})=(OA_{1}O^{\top},\dots,OA_{d}O^{\top}).

Orbits of this action are the simultaneously similar tuples of matrices, whose structure is known to be intricate
[
36
,
74
,
62
]
.
We note that the action of
\mathrm{O}(n)
can also be defined on
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{Lie}}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))
, by simultaneously conjugating all elements of a subspace.

Of particular interest to us is the double quotient
\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{Lie}}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))/\mathrm{O}(d)/\mathrm{O}(n)
, the
d
-subalgebras up to right-multiplication and conjugation.
We do not know whether a simple description of it exists.
Instead, in the next section, we will modify the definition of
\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{Lie}}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))
, adapting it to a fixed Lie group, and showing that its structure becomes tractable.
Namely, the quotient is in correspondence with the orbit-equivalence classes of representations of the group.

Last, it will be convenient to give the Grassmann and Stiefel varieties a distance.
In this paper, we choose to work with the following ones.
Let
E
denote any vector space.
If
(A_{1},\dots,A_{d})
and
(B_{1},\dots,B_{d})
are two orthonormal families of
E
, we simply consider their Frobenius norm
(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|A_{i}-B_{i}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}})^{1/2}
.
If
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
denote the linear subspaces they span, we consider the distance

\big\|\Pi\big[\mathcal{A}\big]-\Pi\big[\mathcal{B}\big]\big\|_{\mathrm{F}},

(5)

where
\Pi\big[\cdot\big]
denotes the orthogonal projection on a subspace, seen as
\dim(E)\times\dim(E)
matrix.
The following equivalent formulations will turn out handy later in the paper.

Lemma 2.9
.

Given
(A_{i})_{i=1}^{d}
and
(B_{i})_{i=1}^{d}
in
\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{Lie}}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))
, with
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
their span, it holds

\big\|\Pi\big[\mathcal{A}\big]-\Pi\big[\mathcal{B}\big]\big\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}=2\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\bigg(1-\langle A_{i},B_{j}\rangle^{2}\bigg)=2\sum_{i=1}^{d}\bigg(1-\big\|\Pi\big[\mathcal{B}\big](A_{i})\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\bigg).

Moreover, when
d=1
, we have

\big\|\Pi\big[\mathcal{A}\big]-\Pi\big[\mathcal{B}\big]\big\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}=\min\big\{2\big\|A_{1}-B_{1}\big\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}},~2\big\|A_{1}+B_{1}\big\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}\big\}.

Let us clarify that, in the equation above, the first Frobenius norm is that of
\mathrm{M}_{n(n-1)/2}(\mathbb{R})
, while the second one is that of
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, even though it is similarly denoted.
We also point out that this lemma is not specific to
\mathfrak{so}(n)
, and holds for any Euclidean vector space.

2.3.2 Stiefel and Grassmann varieties of pushforward Lie algebras

In this paper, we will not only require working with Lie algebra structures, but more specifically with those coming from representations of a compact Lie group.
To this end, we fix a compact Lie group
G
of dimension
d
, and introduce the following variations of
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{Lie}}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))
and
\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{Lie}}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))
:

•

\mathcal{G}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))
, the set consisting of those elements
\mathfrak{h}\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{Lie}}(d,\mathfrak{so}(n))
for which there exists an orthogonal and almost-faithful representation
\phi\colon G\rightarrow\mathrm{SO}(n)
such that
\mathfrak{h}=\mathrm{d}\phi(\mathfrak{g})
.

•

\mathcal{V}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))
, the set consisting of the orthonormal bases of elements in
\mathcal{G}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))
.

As an example, using the notations of Example
2.2
, we can describe
\mathcal{G}(\mathrm{SO}(2),\mathfrak{so}(2m))
as the set of lines of
\mathfrak{so}(2m)
generated by matrices equivalent to
\mathrm{diag}(L(a_{1}),\dots,L(a_{m}))
for some
(a_{1},\dots,a_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\setminus\{0\}
.
In other words, this is the set of ‘rational lines’ on the
m
-torus
T^{m}
.

It is worth stressing a few facts.
First, given two distinct Lie groups
G
and
G^{\prime}
, it is possible that their varieties intersect.
For example,
\mathcal{G}(\mathrm{SO}(3),\mathfrak{so}(n))\subset\mathcal{G}(\mathrm{SU}(2),\mathfrak{so}(n))
for all integer
n
, with equality when
n\leq 3
.
Besides, we note that
\mathcal{G}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))
can be empty, precisely when
G
does not admit almost-faithful representations in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
This is the case for
T^{d}
when
n<2d
.
Moreover, we point out that
\mathcal{V}(T^{d},\mathfrak{so}(n))
is made of
d
-frames of commuting skew-symmetric matrices.
Taking their exponential yields a frame of commuting matrices of
\mathrm{SO}(n)
.
Such objects have received a certain attention, due to their connection to quantum field theories in Physics
[
6
,
3
,
86
]
.
However, and as a last comment, it is not possible to obtain all the commuting frames of
\mathrm{SO}(n)
via this procedure.
Indeed, following the example of
\mathrm{SO}(2)
in the previous paragraph, the exponential of
\mathcal{V}(\mathrm{SO}(2),\mathfrak{so}(2n))
will only contain ‘rational’ orthogonal matrices.

Just as before,
\mathrm{O}(d)
acts by right multiplication on
\mathcal{V}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))
, and the quotient set is

{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathrm{O}(d)}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\mathcal{V}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\mathrm{O}(d)}$}}{\raisebox{2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{V}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{O}(d)}$}}{\raisebox{1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{O}(d)}$}}}\simeq\mathcal{G}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n)).

Besides,
\mathrm{O}(n)
also acts on
\mathcal{V}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))
and
\mathcal{G}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))
by simultaneous conjugation, but now this action carries another interpretation: two Lie algebras are conjugated if and only if they are pushforwards of
orbit-equivalent
representations of
G
, as defined in Section
2.2.3
.
Consequently, the quotient set
\mathcal{G}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))/\mathrm{O}(n)
is in correspondence with the orbit-equivalence classes of pushforward algebras of (orthogonal and almost-faithful) representations of
G
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

In order to describe these spaces more explicitly, we wish to fix a representative for each equivalence classes.
To do so, we first fix a basis
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{d}
of
\mathfrak{g}
.
We also consider
\mathrm{Irr}(G)
, an arbitrary choice of an orthogonal representative for each equivalence class of irreducible representation of
G
.
For all
\phi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G)
, the pushforward algebra
\mathrm{d}\phi(\mathfrak{g})
admits the following basis:

B^{\phi}=\big(\mathrm{d}\phi(e_{1}),\dots,\mathrm{d}\phi(e_{d})\big).

We apply Gram–Schmidt orthogonalization to obtain an element of
\mathcal{V}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))
.
Next, we consider the
p
-tuples
(\phi_{k})_{k=1}^{p}
of elements of
\mathrm{Irr}(G)
, for some integer
p\geq 1
, whose direct sum
\phi=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{p}\phi_{k}
has dimension
n
and is almost-faithful.
We denote by
\mathrm{OrbRep}(G,n)
the set consisting of an arbitrary choice of representative for each orbit-equivalence class of such representations
\phi
.
Last, we define
\mathfrak{orb}(G,n)
as the set of their tuples of pushforward Lie algebras:

\mathfrak{orb}(G,n)=\big\{\big(B^{\phi_{1}},\dots,B^{\phi_{p}}\big)\mid(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{p})\in\mathrm{OrbRep}(G,n)\big\}.

(6)

Based on
\mathfrak{orb}(G,n)
, all the operations previously mentioned can be made explicit:

•

(direct sum)
If
\{\phi_{k}\}_{k=1}^{p}
are irreducible representations with
\sum_{k=1}^{p}\dim(\phi_{k})=n
, then their direct sum
\phi=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{p}\phi_{k}
is a representation of
G
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
A basis
B^{\phi}=(A^{\phi}_{1},\dots,A^{\phi}_{d})
of
\mathrm{d}\phi(\mathfrak{g})
can be obtained from the
B^{\phi_{k}}
’s by putting

A^{\phi}_{i}=\mathrm{diag}\big(B^{\phi_{1}}_{i},\dots,B^{\phi_{p}}_{i}\big).

•

(right-multiplication)
If
P=(P_{i,j})
is a matrix in
\mathrm{O}(d)
, then the
d
-tuple

B^{\phi}\cdot P=\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{d}P_{j,1}A^{\phi}_{j},\dots,\sum_{j=1}^{d}P_{j,d}A^{\phi}_{j}\Big)
is a new basis for
\mathrm{d}\phi(\mathfrak{g})
.

•

(conjugation)
If
O=(O_{i,j})
is a matrix in
\mathrm{O}(n)
, then the
d
-tuple

O\cdot B^{\phi}=\big(OA^{\phi}_{1}O^{\top},\dots,OA^{\phi}_{d}O^{\top}\big)
is a basis for the conjugate Lie algebra
O\mathrm{d}\phi(\mathfrak{g})O^{\top}
.

As a consequence of the aforementioned considerations, any pushforward Lie algebra of an orthogonal and almost-faithful representation of
G
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
can be obtained from these operations.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
G
be a compact Lie group of dimension
d
and
n\geq 1
and integer.
For any orthogonal and almost-faithful representation
\phi\colon G\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, and any orthonormal basis
(A_{1},\dots,A_{d})
of its pushforward Lie algebra
\mathrm{d}\phi(\mathfrak{g})
, there exists an integer
p\geq 1
, a
p
-tuple
(B^{1},\dots,B^{p})\in\mathfrak{orb}(G,n)
and two matrices
O\in\mathrm{O}(n)
and
P\in\mathrm{O}(d)
such that, for all
i\in[1\dots d]
,

A_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}P_{j,i}O\mathrm{diag}\big(B^{k}_{j}\big)_{k=1}^{p}O^{\top}.

In other words, we have
\mathcal{V}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))=\mathrm{O}(n)\cdot\mathfrak{orb}(G,n)\cdot\mathrm{O}(d)
.

This lemma gives an algorithmic procedure to generate all orthonormal bases of pushforward Lie algebras of
G
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
It will be put in practice in Section
3.4
to solve a minimization problem over
\mathcal{V}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))
.
Besides, we draw the reader’s attention to the fact that the formulation
\mathcal{V}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))=\mathrm{O}(n)\cdot\mathfrak{irr}^{n}(G)\cdot\mathrm{O}(d)
has some degree of duplication. Indeed, given
(B^{1},\cdots,B^{p})\in\mathfrak{orb}(G,n)
, there may exist several
O\in\mathrm{O}(n)
, or
P\in\mathrm{O}(d)
, with the same action on it.

3
Description of the algorithm

We now present our algorithm for detecting Lie group representations from orbits.
It takes as an input a finite point cloud
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a compact Lie group
G
, and returns a representation of
G
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for which
X
is likely to lie on one of its orbits.
Three additional parameters must be provided: an integer
l
and a real number
r>0
, to estimate tangent spaces, and a real number
\epsilon>0
, to perform dimension reduction.
Moroever, when the group
G
is Abelian, an additional integer parameter
\omega_{\mathrm{max}}
must be provided, in order to limit the infinite set of non-equivalent representations of
G
that are tested by the algorithm.
While our algorithm can be applied to any point cloud, this section is best understood by imagining that it lies on or near the orbit of a representation of
G
. Besides, this assumption is crucial to the theoretical results we will present in Section
5
.
Thus, throughout this section, we keep in mind the following model:

Model.

Let
G
be a compact Lie group of dimension
d
, with a representation
\phi
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, potentially non-orthogonal.
The Lie algebra of
G
is denoted as
\mathfrak{g}
, and the pushforward as
\mathfrak{h}=\mathrm{d}\phi(\mathfrak{g})
. Let
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an orbit of this representation,
l
its dimension, and
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{N}\}
a finite sample of points close to or included in
\mathcal{O}
.

The representation, pushforward Lie algebra, and orbit estimated by our algorithm will be denoted respectively
\widehat{\phi}
,
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
, and
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{x}
, for some
x\in X
.

3.1
Overview

Let us go over the big-picture justification behind the algorithm, whose details will be further explained in the next sections.
It works in four steps:

Step 1:

Orthonormalization
(Section
3.2
)
First, we normalize the orbit
\mathcal{O}
, so as to make
\phi
an orthogonal representation.
Following Section
2.2.1
, there exists a positive-definite matrix
M
such that the translated orbit
M\mathcal{O}
lies in the unit sphere.
We find it as the square root of the Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverse of covariance matrix:

M=\sqrt{\Sigma[X]^{+}}~~~~~\mathrm{where}~~~~~\Sigma[X]=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}x_{i}^{\top}.

However, when
\Sigma[X]
is singular, or close to, this procedure can lead to large numerical errors.
This is the case when
\mathcal{O}
does not span
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
We solve this issue with dimension reduction, via Principal Component Analysis (PCA): with
\Pi_{\Sigma[X]}^{>\epsilon}
be the projection matrix on the eigenspaces of eigenvalue greater than
\epsilon
, where
\epsilon
is a parameter, we compute

\widetilde{X}=\sqrt{\Sigma[X]^{+}}\cdot\Pi_{\Sigma[X]}^{>\epsilon}\cdot X.

(7)

In order to simplify the next parts, we will write
X
instead of
\widetilde{X}
.

Step 2:

Lie-PCA
(Section
3.3
)
The second step of our algorithm consists in estimating
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
, the Lie algebra of the symmetry group of
\mathcal{O}
(introduced in Section
2.1.4
), using the Lie-PCA algorithm
[
15
]
.
In this context, we see
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
as a linear subspace of the matrices
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
.
Lie-PCA is based on the operator
\Lambda\colon\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
defined as

\Lambda(A)=\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}\widehat{\Pi}\big[\mathrm{N}_{x_{i}}X\big]\cdot A\cdot\Pi\big[\langle x_{i}\rangle\big],

(8)

where the
\widehat{\Pi}\big[\mathrm{N}_{x_{i}}X\big]
’s are estimations of the projection matrices on the normal spaces
\mathrm{N}_{x_{i}}\mathcal{O}
.
In practice, we find them as
\Pi_{x_{i}}^{l,r}[X]
, the projection matrix on the top
n-l
eigenvectors of the local covariance matrices.
As we will prove in Section
5.2
, the kernel of
\Lambda
is approximately
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
.
That is, matrices
A\in\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
can be estimated as those for which
\|\Lambda(A)\|/\|A\|
is small.

Step 3:

Closest Lie algebra
(Section
3.4
)
For the third step, we seek the pushforward Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}=\mathrm{d}\phi(\mathfrak{g})
. It is included
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
.
Consequently, an orthonormal basis
(A_{1},\dots,A_{d})
of
\mathfrak{h}
must belong approximately to the kernel of
\Lambda
, thus we are invited to consider the program

\arg\min\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|\Lambda(A_{i})\|^{2}\;\;\;\text{s.t. }(A_{1},\dots,A_{d})\in\mathcal{V}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{so}(n)),

where
\mathcal{V}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))
is the Stiefel variety of Lie subalgebras of
\mathfrak{so}(n)
pushforward of
G
.
Concretely, we implement this problem using the decomposition of Lemma
2.10
: any element of
\mathcal{V}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))
can be obtained from a
p
-tuple of irreps of
G
, yielding

\arg\min\sum_{i=1}^{d}\bigg\|\Lambda\bigg(O\mathrm{diag}\big(B^{k}_{j}\big)_{k=1}^{p}O^{\top}\bigg)\bigg\|^{2}\;\;\;\text{s.t. }\begin{cases}(B^{1},\dots,B^{p})\in\mathfrak{orb}(G,n),\\
O\in\mathrm{O}(n).\end{cases}

(9)

This program consists in an optimization over
\mathrm{O}(n)
for each
p
-tuple
(B^{1},\dots,B^{p})
.
An explicit description of
\mathfrak{orb}(G,n)
is necessary; that we give for
\mathrm{SO}(2)
,
T^{d}
,
\mathrm{SU}(2)
and
\mathrm{SO}(3)
in Section
4
.
We denote
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
the subalgebra spanned by a minimizer.

Step 3’:

We also propose a variation of
Step 3
, valid when
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
, i.e., when
G\simeq\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
.
Let
(A_{1},\dots,A_{d})
be the bottom
d
eigenvectors of
\Lambda
. They are close to
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
, but may not form a Lie algebra.

Step 3
’ consists in projecting it to the closest Lie algebra.
Using the distance on the Grassmannian defined in Equation (
5
), we naturally consider

\arg\min\big\|\Pi\big[\langle A_{i}\rangle_{i=1}^{d}\big]-\Pi\big[\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}\big]\big\|~~~~\text{s.t. }~~~~\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}\in\mathcal{G}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n)),

where
\mathcal{G}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))
is the Grassmann variety of Lie subalgebras of
\mathfrak{so}(n)
that are derived from
G
, embedded in
\mathrm{M}_{n(n-1)/2}(\mathbb{R})
.
Using Lemma
2.10
, it can be split into

\arg\min\big\|\Pi\big[\langle A_{i}\rangle_{i=1}^{d}\big]-\Pi\big[\langle O\mathrm{diag}(B^{k}_{i})_{k=1}^{p}O^{\top}\rangle_{i=1}^{d}\big]\big\|\;\;\;\text{s.t. }\begin{cases}(B^{k})_{k=1}^{p}\in\mathfrak{orb}(G,n),\\
O\in\mathrm{O}(n).\end{cases}

(10)

This variation of
Step 3
is closer to the original formulation of Lie-PCA, but relies critically on the assumption
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
, not satisfied in general.

Step 4:

Distance to orbit
(Section
3.5
)
Finally, we check whether the previously estimated Lie subalgebra
\widehat{h}
yields indeed an orbit close to the input
X
.
To do so, we pick an arbitrary point
x\in X
, build its orbit
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{x}
and compute the non-symmetric Hausdorff distance:

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{H}}\big(X|\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{x}\big)~~~~~\mathrm{where}~~~~~\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{x}=\big\{\exp(A)x\mid A\in\widehat{h}\big\},

(11)

This quantity can be approximated by sampling
\widehat{h}
.
In the case where
X
contains anomalous points, the Hausdorff distance may be large, and measure-theoretical distances are better suited.
We build instead a measure
\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}
and compute the Wasserstein distance

\mathrm{W}_{2}\big(\mu_{X},\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}\big)~~~~~\mathrm{where}~~~~~\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{x_{i}}},~~~~~~~~~~~~

(12)

and where
\mu_{X}
is the empirical measure on
X
, and each
\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{x_{i}}}
the uniform measure on
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{x_{i}}
.

The undetailed version of the algorithm is exposed in Algorithm
3.1
.
In this section, we will focus on describing the precise implementation of each step.
The theoretical analysis of the algorithm will only be given later, in Section
5
.
In order to ease the navigation in this paper, we indicate in the following table the results that justify each step.

Step 1

Step 2

Step 3
&
Step 3
’

Step 4

Algorithm
3.1

Prop.
5.7

Prop.
5.15

Lemma
5.17

Prop.
5.19

Th.
5.21

Moreover, throughout this section, we will illustrate the algorithm on a concrete dataset, presented in Example
3.1
.

Example 3.1
.

We consider a set
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
of
300
points sampled close to the curve

\mathcal{O}=\big\{(\cos t,2\sin t,\cos 4t,\sin 4t)\mid t\in[0,2\pi)\big\}.

This is generated by sampling
t\in[0,2\pi)
uniformly and then adding a slight Gaussian noise (standard deviation
\sigma=0.01
). The data is visualized in Figure
1
.
The curve
\mathcal{O}
is the orbit of the point
(1,0,1,0)^{\top}
for the representation
\phi\colon\mathrm{SO}(2)\rightarrow\mathrm{M}_{4}(\mathbb{R})
defined as

t\mapsto\mathrm{diag}\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}\cos t&-2\sin t\\
(1/2)\sin t&\cos t\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\cos 4t&-\sin 4t\\
\sin 4t&\cos 4t\end{pmatrix}\bigg).

This representation is not orthogonal.
However, by removing the two
2
’s, we see that it is equivalent to

\phi_{1}\oplus\phi_{4}
, that is, a sum of rotations with weights
1
and
4
(see Section
2.2.2
).
The corresponding pushforward subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
is the subspace of
\mathfrak{so}(4)
spanned by the matrix

A=\mathrm{diag}\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&-4\\
4&0\end{pmatrix}\bigg)\bigg).

In this example, we expect our algorithm to output
\phi_{1}\oplus\phi_{4}
.

3.2
Step 1
: Orthonormalization

Let us start with
Step 1
, whose objective is to project the data set
X=\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
close to the unit sphere of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, transforming the representation
\phi\colon G\rightarrow\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
into an orthogonal one.
This pre-processing step significantly simplifies the rest of the algorithm, by restricting the analysis to orthogonal representations only.
It is important to note that this ‘projection’ cannot be simply understood as a metric projection on the unit sphere.
Indeed, generally, such an operation does not yield an orthogonal representation, as we show in Example
3.2
.
Instead, the transformation should be found using the following fact: there exists a positive-definite matrix
M
such that the conjugated representation
M\phi M^{-1}
takes values in
\mathrm{O}(n)
, that is, is orthogonal.
Orbits for the latter representation are obtained by left translation by
M
of orbits for the former.

Our construction is based on the
covariance matrix
of
X
, defined as

\Sigma[X]=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}x_{i}^{\top},

and where
x_{i}x_{i}^{\top}
represents the outer product of
x_{i}
by itself.
It is a symmetric
n\times n
matrix.
First of all, we will employ
\Sigma[X]
to reduce the dimension.
More precisely, we use Principal Components Analysis (PCA):
X
is projected on the subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
spanned by the eigenvectors of
\Sigma[X]
of eigenvalue greater than
\epsilon
, where
\epsilon
is a parameter of the algorithm.
The corresponding projection matrix is denoted
\Pi_{\Sigma[X]}^{>\epsilon}
.
The parameter
\epsilon
is supposedly small, so as to project
X
into
\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle
, the subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
spanned by
\mathcal{O}
.
We stress that this pre-processing step has two objectives.
First, it allows for avoiding numerical errors, when computing the pseudo-inverse of
\Sigma[X]
, as we will do below.
Secondly, by working in the subspace spanned by
\mathcal{O}
, and as detailed in Section
2.1.4
, the homomorphism
\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{O})\rightarrow\mathrm{Isom}(\mathcal{O})
becomes injective.
This property will be crucial in
Step 3
, for ensuring the detection of non-trivial actions of
G
in
\mathcal{O}
.

Next, we compute the Moore–Penrose pseudo-inverse
\Sigma[X]^{+}
, and take its square root
\sqrt{\Sigma[X]^{+}}
.
It is well-defined since a positive semi-definite matrix admits a unique square root.
We eventually define the product
M=\sqrt{\Sigma[X]^{+}}\cdot\Pi_{\Sigma[X]}^{>\epsilon}
, and generate the close-to-the-sphere data set
\{Mx_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
, which will be used in the following parts.

Example 3.2
.

We consider the set
X
and the orbit
\mathcal{O}
defined in Example
3.1
.
Projecting
\mathcal{O}
onto the sphere
S^{3}
would yield the set

\bigg\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2+\sin^{2}t}}(\cos t,2\sin t,\cos 4t,\sin 4t)\mid t\in[0,2\pi)\bigg\},

which is not an orbit of an orthogonal representation of
\mathrm{SO}(2)
.
However, the minimization procedure of
Step 1
yields a matrix and projected set approximately equal to

M=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathrm{diag}\big(1,1/2,1,1\big)~~~~\text{and}~~~~\bigg\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\cos t,\sin t,\cos 4t,\sin 4t)\mid t\in[0,2\pi)\bigg\},

which is indeed an orbit of an orthogonal representation.
It can be visualized in Figure
1
.

Figure 1
:
The input point cloud
X
, and its transformation after
Step 1
(both projected in dimension
3
through PCA for visualisation purposes).

3.3
Step 2
: Lie-PCA

For Step 2, we consider the point cloud
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, having applied
Step 1
or not, and wish to find
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
.
We remind the reader that the symmetry group
\mathrm{Sym}(\mathcal{O})
and its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
have been defined in Section
2.1.4
.
This step is based on the work developed in
[
15
]
.

3.3.1 The Lie-PCA operator

Lie-PCA is based on the fact that, for all
x\in\mathcal{O}
,
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
is contained in the set

S_{x}\mathcal{O}=\{A\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\mid Ax\in\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{O}\},

where
\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{O}
denotes the tangent space of the manifold
\mathcal{O}
at
x
.
As we have seen in Equation (
2
),
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
is equal to the intersection
\cap_{x\in\mathcal{O}}S_{x}\mathcal{O}
.
Now, having access only to the point cloud
X
, the authors of
[
15
]
propose to estimate
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
via

\bigcap_{i=1}^{N}S_{x_{i}}\mathcal{O}=\ker\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Pi\big[{(S_{x_{i}}\mathcal{O})^{\bot}}\big]\bigg),

where the projections
\Pi\big[(S_{x_{i}}\mathcal{O})^{\bot}\big]
are seen as operators on
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
.
In practice, the
S_{x_{i}}\mathcal{O}
are unknown, and a robust estimation is to be found.
It is shown that
\Pi_{(S_{x_{i}}\mathcal{O})^{\bot}}
is equal to

A\mapsto\Pi\big[\mathrm{N}_{x_{i}}\mathcal{O}\big]\cdot A\cdot\Pi\big[\langle x_{i}\rangle\big],

where
\mathrm{N}_{x_{i}}\mathcal{O}
is the normal space of
\mathcal{O}
at
x_{i}
,
\langle x_{i}\rangle
is the line spanned by
x_{i}
, and
\Pi\big[\mathrm{N}_{x_{i}}\mathcal{O}\big]
and
\Pi\big[\langle x_{i}\rangle\big]
denotes the projection matrices on these subspaces.
By denoting
\widehat{\Pi}\big[\mathrm{N}_{x_{i}}X\big]
an estimation of
\Pi_{\mathrm{N}_{x_{i}}\mathcal{O}}
computed from the observation
X
, we replace the previous operator with

A\mapsto\widehat{\Pi}\big[\mathrm{N}_{x_{i}}X\big]\cdot A\cdot\Pi\big[\langle x_{i}\rangle\big].

Further details on the estimation of normal spaces are provided below.
Combining all the aforementioned aspects, a reliable estimation of
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
can be obtained by computing the kernel of the operator
\Lambda\colon\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
defined as

\Lambda(A)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\widehat{\Pi}\big[\mathrm{N}_{x_{i}}X\big]\cdot A\cdot\Pi\big[\langle x_{i}\rangle\big].

More accurately,
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
should be sought as the linear subspace of
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
spanned by the eigenvectors associated with almost-zero eigenvalues of
\Lambda
.
We stress that
\Lambda
is a symmetric operator, hence diagonalizable when seen as a
n^{2}\times n^{2}
matrix.

3.3.2 Estimation of normal spaces

We now turn to the estimations
\widehat{\Pi}\big[\mathrm{N}_{x_{i}}X\big]
of the normal spaces
\Pi\big[\mathrm{N}_{x_{i}}\mathcal{O}\big]
.
First, we note that the problem of estimation of normal spaces is equivalent to the one for tangent spaces, since we have the relation

\Pi\big[\mathrm{T}_{x_{i}}\mathcal{O}\big]+\Pi\big[\mathrm{N}_{x_{i}}\mathcal{O}\big]=I

with
I
the identity matrix.
Indeed,
\mathrm{T}_{x_{i}}\mathcal{O}
and
\mathrm{N}_{x_{i}}\mathcal{O}
are complementary orthogonal subspaces. Now, estimating tangent spaces can be done through the common technique of
local PCA

[
88
,
52
,
1
,
60
]
.
Given a real number
r>0
, called the scale parameter, this technique first computes the
local covariance matrix
of
X
at scale
r
and centered at
x_{i}
, defined as

\Sigma_{x_{i}}^{r}[X]=\frac{1}{|Y|}\sum_{y\in Y}(y-x_{i})(y-x_{i})^{\top},

(13)

where
Y=\{y\in X\mid\|y-x_{i}\|\leq r\}
is the set of input points at distance at most
r
from
x_{i}
.
Now, given the dimension
l
of
\mathcal{O}
, known in advance or estimated, we estimate
\mathrm{T}_{x_{i}}\mathcal{O}
via the space spanned by the
l
bottom eigenvectors of
\Sigma_{x_{i}}^{r}[X]
.
In what follows, we denote by
\Pi_{x_{i}}^{l,r}[X]
the projection on this space.
We eventually consider the estimator

\widehat{\Pi}\big[\mathrm{N}_{x_{i}}X\big]=I-\Pi_{x_{i}}^{l,r}[X].

Due to the popularity of local PCA, probabilistic guarantees for this procedure can be found in several works, such as those cited above.
Moreover, closely related variations have been studied, obtained by weighting the matrix
\Sigma_{x_{i}}^{r}[X]
, or by using
k
-neighbors
[
79
,
15
]
.
All of these works, however, state their results in probability, and not as deterministic inequalities, as we will need when analyzing our algorithm.
This will be remedied in Section
5.2.3
, which contains deterministic stability results for
\Pi_{x_{i}}^{r,l}[X]
with respect to Wasserstein distance.

In actual implementations, the set
Y
is obtained using the binary tree algorithm
[
10
]
from the Python library
scikit-learn

[
71
]
.
Besides, we stress that the accuracy of local PCA, in addition to its dependence on the geometry of
\mathcal{O}
and the density of
X
, is highly affected by the parameter
r
, since too small values imply large variance, whereas bigger ones give biased estimations.
We will derive in Lemmas
5.12
and
5.13
a theoretical range in which
r
should be chosen, although, in practice, there is little
ad hoc
guidance in the choice.

3.3.3 Implementation

As presented in the original paper, the last step of Lie-PCA consists in computing the linear subspace of
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
spanned by the
l
bottom eigenvectors of
\Lambda
, offering an estimation of
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
.
This procedure, however, is not suited to our context, since we seek
\mathfrak{h}
, which is only a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
.
An example is given by the unit sphere
S^{3}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
, which is an orbit of a representation of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
, but whose symmetry group is
\mathrm{SO}(4)
, of higher dimension.
In this case, the inclusion of
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{su}(2)
in
\mathfrak{sym}(S^{3})=\mathfrak{so}(4)
is not an equality.
Consequently, instead of computing the eigenvectors of
\Lambda
, we simply compute the values it takes on the canonical basis of
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, and save them in a
n^{2}\times n^{2}
matrix.
This will allow us, in
