\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\iff\bigg(2\bigg(\frac{\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}\bigg)^{1/2}+\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)\cdot\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)<\frac{1}{2}.

(26)

By combining Lemma
5.1
with this second inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]-\Sigma[\nu]\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(2\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]^{1/2}+\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)\bigg)\cdot\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)<\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}{2}.

(27)

Denote by
m
the dimension of
\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle
, and consider the projections
\Pi_{\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]}^{m}
and
\Pi^{m}_{\Sigma[\nu]}
on the top
m
eigenvectors of
\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]
and
\Sigma[\nu]
.
Note that the kernel of
\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]
is
\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle^{\bot}
, and its
m^{\mathrm{th}}
eigengap is
\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}
.
With Equation (
27
), we deduce that the hypothesis of Lemma
5.3
is satisfied.
It yields

\displaystyle\big\|\Pi_{\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]}^{m}-\Pi^{m}_{\Sigma[\nu]}\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}\bigg(2\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]^{1/2}+\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)\bigg)\cdot\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu).

We will now identify the matrices
\Pi_{\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]}^{m}
and
\Pi^{m}_{\Sigma[\nu]}
.
Since the kernel of
\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]
is
\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle^{\bot}
, we have
\Pi_{\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]}^{m}=\Pi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\big]
.
Besides, using Equation (
27
) and the assumption
\epsilon/\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}\leq 1/2
, we have that

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}-\big\|\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]-\Sigma[\nu]\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}>\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}-\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}/2\geq\epsilon.

Similarly, Equation (
27
) and the assumption
\big(2\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]^{1/2}+\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)\big)\cdot\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)<\epsilon
yields

\displaystyle 0+\big\|\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]-\Sigma[\nu]\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\big(2\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]^{1/2}+\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)\big)\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)<\epsilon.

Consequently, by Weyl’s theorem for the stability of eigenvalues,
\Sigma[\nu]
admits exactly
m
eigenvalues greater or equal to
\epsilon
.
Hence, we have
\Pi^{m}_{\Sigma[\nu]}=\Pi_{\Sigma[\nu]}^{>\epsilon}
.
As a consequence of these equalities, we obtain Equation (
25
).
Now, to prove the statement of the proposition, we apply the triangle inequality of the Wasserstein distance on the pushforward measures
\Pi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\big]\mu_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\Pi_{\Sigma[\nu]}^{>\epsilon}\nu
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{2}\big(\Pi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\big]\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\Pi_{\Sigma[\nu]}^{>\epsilon}\nu\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{W}_{2}\big(\Pi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\big]\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\Pi_{\Sigma[\nu]}^{>\epsilon}\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\big)+\mathrm{W}_{2}\big(\Pi_{\Sigma[\nu]}^{>\epsilon}\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\Pi_{\Sigma[\nu]}^{>\epsilon}\nu\big)

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\Pi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\big]-\Pi_{\Sigma[\nu]}^{>\epsilon}\big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\cdot{\mathbb{E}[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|^{2}]}^{1/2}+\big\|\Pi_{\Sigma[\nu]}^{>\epsilon}\big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\cdot\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu).

The second term is equal to
\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)
.
In order to bound the first one, we use the loose bound
\mathbb{E}[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|^{2}]=\Tr\big(\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]\big)\leq n\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}
, seen in Equation (
24
).
Combined with Equation (
25
), we get

\big\|\Pi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\big]-\Pi_{\Sigma[\nu]}^{>\epsilon}\big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\cdot{\mathbb{E}[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|^{2}]}^{1/2}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2n}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg(\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)\bigg(2\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]^{1/2}+\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)\bigg)\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu).

We simplify it with
\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]\leq\mathbb{E}[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|^{2}]\leq n\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}
and
\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)\leq\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}/\sqrt{2}\leq\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}/\sqrt{2}
:

\big\|\Pi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\big]-\Pi_{\Sigma[\nu]}^{>\epsilon}\big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\cdot{\mathbb{E}[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|^{2}]}^{1/2}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2n}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg(\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)\cdot\bigg(2\sqrt{n}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\bigg)\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}\cdot\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu).

To conclude, we gather the terms and use the inequality
\sqrt{2n}\cdot\big(2\sqrt{n}+1/\sqrt{2}\big)\leq 3n+2
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{2}\big(\Pi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\big]\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\Pi_{\Sigma[\nu]}^{>\epsilon}\nu\big)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2n}\cdot\bigg(2\sqrt{n}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\bigg)\cdot\bigg(\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}\bigg)\cdot\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)+\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)

\displaystyle\leq 3(n+1)\cdot\bigg(\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}\bigg)\cdot\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu).\qed

We point out that Proposition
5.4
has an important consequence in practice: performing PCA on
X
will give a new point cloud that is again close to the orbit of a representation.
Indeed, if
\phi
denotes the representation that generates
\mathcal{O}
, then the span of
\mathcal{O}
is an invariant subspace of
\phi
, hence the projection
\Pi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\big](\mathcal{O})
is still an orbit of a representation.

Besides, we note that the assumptions of the proposition are valid, provided that
\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)
is small enough, and that the parameter
\epsilon
is chosen in accordance with
\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)
.
In practice, however, these conditions are hardly verifiable, since the quantities
\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}
,
\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}
and
\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]
are unknown.
In order to address this issue, we could try to estimate these quantities using the observation
X
, in a statistical fashion, although we did not go further in this direction.

Remark 5.5
.

The stability of PCA, expressed in Proposition
5.4
above, involves the terms

\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}},~~~~\frac{\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}~~~~\mathrm{and}~~~~~\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}.

They correspond respectively to the homogeneity of
\mathcal{O}
, its deviation from orthogonality, and the quality of the sample
\nu
.
Moreover, they are all
invariant under rescaling
.
That is, multiplying the input
\mu_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\nu
by a fixed positive constant won’t modify their value.
This is an important property, showing that the algorithm is not affected by the scale, hence works with intrinsic geometric features of the input.
We will ensure this property in all the results to come.

5.1.2 Orthonormalization

The second part of
Step 1
consists in computing
M=\sqrt{\Sigma[X]^{+}}
, the square root of the Moore–Penrose pseudo-inverse of the covariance matrix, and in translating
X
by
M
.
This step actually implements the orthogonalization of representations, a standard practice when working with Lie groups.
In fact, along the way, we will implement a stronger result:
MX
will be close to a homogeneous orbit, as defined in Section
5.1.1
.

We recall that a representation
\phi\colon G\rightarrow\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
is orthogonal if it takes values in
\mathrm{O}(n)
, i.e., if the relation
\big\langle\phi(g)x,\phi(g)y\big\rangle=\langle x,y\rangle
holds for all
g\in G
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where we consider the usual Euclidean inner product.
As recalled in Section
2.2.1
, for any representation
\phi
of a compact Lie group, there exists a positive-definite matrix
M\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
such that the conjugated representation
M\phi M^{-1}
is orthogonal.
Let us write down the classic construction of such a matrix.
We define an inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
via

\langle x,y\rangle_{G}=\int\big\langle\phi(g)x,\phi(g)y\big\rangle\mathrm{d}\mu_{G}(g),

with
\mu_{G}
the Haar measure.
By construction, the representation is orthogonal with respect to this inner product.
Let
K
be the matrix of scalar products of the canonical basis vectors
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
It is a symmetric positive-definite matrix, and satisfy the relation
\langle x,y\rangle_{G}=\langle Kx,y\rangle
.
By denoting
M=K^{1/2}
a square root, the relation becomes
\langle x,y\rangle_{G}=\langle Mx,My\rangle
, and we see that the conjugated representation is orthogonal with respect to the usual inner product:

\displaystyle\big\langle M\phi(g)M^{-1}x,M\phi(g)M^{-1}y\big\rangle=\big\langle\phi(g)M^{-1}x,\phi(g)M^{-1}y\big\rangle_{G}=\big\langle M^{-1}x,M^{-1}y\big\rangle_{G}=\big\langle x,y\big\rangle.

In the context of this article, this construction of the matrix
M
cannot be used, since we do not have access to the representation
\phi
, but only to a sample
X
of an orbit
\mathcal{O}
of it.
However, it can be easily adapted.
Indeed, with
\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]
the covariance matrix of
\mathcal{O}
, and any matrix
M\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, we observe that Equation (
23
) yields

\displaystyle\Sigma[M\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]=\int\big(Mx)\big(Mx)^{\top}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{O}}(x)=M\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]M^{\top}.

We remark that the choice of
M=\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{-1/2}
, the square root of the inverse of
\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]
, yields the particularly simple covariance matrix
\Sigma[M\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]=I
.
Moreover, in this case, the conjugated representation
M\phi M^{-1}
is orthogonal.
Indeed, by definition of
\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]
, we have, for all
g\in G
,

\phi(g)^{-1}\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]=\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]\phi(g)^{-1}\top,

We deduce that
M\phi(g)M^{-1}
is an orthogonal matrix.
This justifies our choice of
M
.
In addition, since
\Sigma[M\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]=I
, the ratio of its top and bottom eigenvalues is
1
, hence
M\mathcal{O}
is a homogeneous orbit, as defined in Section
5.1.1
.
We could say that the orbit has been ‘orthonormalized’.

We now discuss the concrete implementation of this procedure, when the input is a point cloud
X
, or more generally a measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
We suppose that PCA pre-processing has been applied successfully to
\mathcal{O}
and
X
, hence that
\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle=\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
In particular,
\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]
is non-singular.
We aim at showing that the square root
M=\Sigma[\nu]^{-1/2}
allows to make the underlying representation orthogonal.
To do so, we will follow the same strategy as we did when studying PCA: deriving an upper bound on the distance between
\Sigma[\nu]
and
\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]
, expressed with the Wasserstein distance, and deducing that the output of our algorithm is consistent.
In opposition to PCA, the following result does not involve projection on eigenspaces, and is more straightforward to prove.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the orbit of a representation, potentially non-orthogonal, that spans
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Let
\mu_{\mathcal{O}}
be its uniform measure, and
\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}^{2},\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}
the top and bottom eigenvalues of
\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]
.
Let
\nu
be a measure, and
\Sigma[\nu]
its covariance matrix.
We suppose that
\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)
satisfies the first hypothesis of Proposition
5.4
.
Then
\Sigma[\nu]
is positive-definite, and we have a bound

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{2}\bigg(\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{-1/2}\mu_{\mathcal{O}},~\Sigma[\nu]^{-1/2}\nu\bigg)

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\leq\big(\sqrt{2n}+2\big)\bigg(\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg(2\bigg(\frac{\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}\bigg)^{1/2}+\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)^{1/2}.

Proof.

The first step of the proof consists in showing the inequality

\displaystyle\big\|\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{-1/2}-\Sigma[\nu]^{-1/2}\big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}\cdot\bigg(2\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]^{1/2}+\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)\bigg)^{1/2}\cdot\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)^{1/2}.

(28)

Let us recall a few relations regarding the operator norm of matrices.
First, the inequality

\|A^{-1}-B^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|A-B\|_{\mathrm{op}}\cdot\|B^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\cdot\|A^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

is valid for any pair of invertible matrices.
This relation comes from applying the submultiplicativity of the operator norm to the equality
A^{-1}-B^{-1}=A^{-1}(B-A)B^{-1}
.
Now, if
A
is a positive definite matrix, then
\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}
is equal to its top eigenvalue.
Consequently,
\|A^{1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{1/2}
and
\|A^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\lambda^{-1}
, with
\lambda
its bottom eigenvalue.
Moreover, it is known that

\|A^{1/2}-B^{1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|A-B\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{1/2}.

Putting these relations together, we deduce

\displaystyle\|A^{-1/2}-B^{-1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\|A^{1/2}-B^{1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\cdot\|A^{-{1/2}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\cdot\|B^{-{1/2}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\|A-B\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{1/2}\cdot\lambda^{-1/2}\cdot(\lambda-\|A-B\|_{\mathrm{op}})^{1/2},

(29)

where we used Weyl’s eigenvalues stability for the last term.
We now apply these results in our context.
In Equation (
27
) of the proof of Proposition
5.4
, we have observed that

\displaystyle\|\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]-\Sigma[\nu]\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\bigg(2\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]^{1/2}+\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)\bigg)\cdot\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)<\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}{2}.

This second inequality, combined with the fact that the eigenvalues of
\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]
are at least
\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}
, shows that
\Sigma[\nu]
is non-singular.
Moreover, the first and second inequality respectively yield

\displaystyle\big\|\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]-\Sigma[\nu]\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(2\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]^{1/2}+\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)\bigg)^{1/2}\cdot\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)^{1/2},

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}-\big\|\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]-\Sigma[\nu]\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\geq\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}-\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}/2=\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}/2.

We eventually bound
\big\|\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{-1/2}-\Sigma[\nu]^{-1/2}\big\|_{\mathrm{op}}
through the inequality of Equation (
29
):

\displaystyle\big\|\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{-1/2}-\Sigma[\nu]^{-1/2}\big\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]-\Sigma[\nu]\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{1/2}\cdot\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}\cdot\big(\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}-\big\|\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]-\Sigma[\nu]\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}\big)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(2\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]^{1/2}+\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)\bigg)^{1/2}\cdot\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)^{1/2}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}.

This is Equation (
28
) previously announced.
Next, in order to prove the inequality of the proposition, we consider the pushforward measures and apply the triangle inequality:

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{2}\big(

\displaystyle\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{-1/2}\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\Sigma[\nu]^{-1/2}\nu\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{W}_{2}\big(\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{-1/2}\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\Sigma[\nu]^{-1/2}\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\big)+\mathrm{W}_{2}\big(\Sigma[\nu]^{-1/2}\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\Sigma[\nu]^{-1/2}\nu\big)

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{-1/2}-\Sigma[\nu]^{-1/2}\big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\cdot{\mathbb{E}[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|^{2}]}^{1/2}+\big\|\Sigma[\nu]^{-1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\cdot\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{-1/2}-\Sigma[\nu]^{-1/2}\big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\bigg({\mathbb{E}[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|^{2}]}^{1/2}+\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)\bigg)+\big\|\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{-1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\cdot\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu),

where we used
\big\|\Sigma[\nu]^{-1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\big\|\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{-1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)
on the last line.
The second term is equal to
\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)/\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}
.
For the first one, we use the inequality
\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)\leq\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}/\sqrt{2}
, coming from the hypothesis, and
\mathbb{E}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|^{2}\big]\leq\Tr{\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]}\leq n\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}
, coming from Equation (
24
), to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|^{2}\big]^{1/2}+\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)\leq\sqrt{n}\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}+\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}{\sqrt{2}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big(\sqrt{2n}+1\big)\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}.

Combined with Equation (
28
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\big\|\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{-\frac{1}{2}}-\Sigma[\nu]^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\big(\mathbb{E}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|^{2}\big]^{1/2}+\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)\big)

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(2\frac{\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]^{1/2}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}+\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)^{1/2}\cdot\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)^{1/2}\cdot\bigg(\sqrt{2n}+1\bigg)\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}.

We collect the terms to conclude:

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{2}\bigg(\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{-1/2}\mu_{\mathcal{O}},~\Sigma[\nu]^{-1/2}\nu\bigg)\leq

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)+\big(\sqrt{2n}+1\big)\bigg(\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg(2\bigg(\frac{\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}\bigg)^{1/2}+\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg).

Simplifying this equation gives the result of the proposition.
∎

We observe that in Proposition
5.6
, the distance
\mathrm{W}_{2}\big(\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{-1/2}\mu_{\mathcal{O}},~\Sigma[\nu]^{-1/2}\nu\big)
does not appear normalized by a constant, as it is the case for the other terms.
This, however, does not contradict the invariance under rescaling, raised in Remark
5.5
.
Indeed, the distance is ‘dimensionless’, i.e., already invariant, since
\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{-1/2}\mu_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\Sigma[\nu]^{-1/2}\nu
are ‘normalized’ measures.

We now gather Propositions
5.4
and
5.6
to obtain our main result for
Step 1
.
It shows that the orthonormalized measure
\sqrt{\Sigma[\nu]^{+}}\Pi_{\Sigma[\nu]}^{>\epsilon}\nu
, output of
Step 1
defined in Equation (
7
), is close to
\sqrt{\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{+}}\Pi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\big]\mu_{\mathcal{O}}
, which is the uniform measure on an orbit of an orthogonal representation that spans the ambient space, as it is wished in this pre-processing step.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the orbit of a representation, potentially non-orthogonal,
\mu_{\mathcal{O}}
its uniform measure,
\Pi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\big]
the projection on its span, and
\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}^{2},\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}
the top and bottom nonzero eigenvalues of
\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]
.
Besides, let
\nu
be a measure,
\Sigma[\nu]
its covariance matrix,
\epsilon>0
and
\Pi_{\Sigma[\nu]}^{>\epsilon}
the projection on the subspace spanned by eigenvectors with eigenvalue at least
\epsilon
.
Suppose

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}<\bigg(\bigg(\frac{\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\bigg)^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}-\bigg(\frac{\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg)\bigg/\bigg(3(n+1)\cdot\bigg(\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}\bigg)\bigg)

\displaystyle\mathrm{and}~~~~~

\displaystyle\bigg(2\bigg(\frac{\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}\bigg)^{1/2}+\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)\cdot\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)<\frac{\epsilon}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Then we have the following bound between the pushforward measures after
Step 1
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{2}\bigg(\sqrt{\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{+}}\Pi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\big]\mu_{\mathcal{O}},~\sqrt{\Sigma[\nu]^{+}}\Pi_{\Sigma[\nu]}^{>\epsilon}\nu\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq 8(n+1)^{3/2}\bigg(\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}^{3}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{3}}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg(\bigg(\frac{\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}\bigg)^{1/2}+\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)^{1/2}.

Proof.

This assumption on
\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)
is stronger than in Proposition
5.4
. Thus its result holds:

\mathrm{W}_{2}\bigg(\Pi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\big]\mu_{\mathcal{O}},~\Pi_{\Sigma[\nu]}^{>\epsilon}\nu\bigg)\bigg/\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}\leq\underbrace{3(n+1)\cdot\bigg(\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}\bigg)}_{\alpha}\cdot\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg).

Still using the assumption on
\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)
, we deduce that

\mathrm{W}_{2}\bigg(\Pi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\big]\mu_{\mathcal{O}},~\Pi_{\Sigma[\nu]}^{>\epsilon}\nu\bigg)\bigg/\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}\leq\bigg(\frac{\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\bigg)^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}-\bigg(\frac{\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}\bigg)^{1/2}.

Hence we can apply Proposition
5.6
on
\Pi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\big]\mu_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\Pi_{\Sigma[\nu]}^{>\epsilon}\nu
.
They have been projected into the subspaces
\mathcal{O}
and
X
span, thus we can identify the inverse of the covariance matrices of the projected measures with the Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverse of the initial measures, therefore

\Sigma[\Pi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\big]\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{-1/2}=\sqrt{\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{+}}\Pi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\big]~~~~~\mathrm{and}~~~~~\Sigma[\Pi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\big]\nu]^{-1/2}=\sqrt{\Sigma[\nu]^{+}}\Pi_{\Sigma[\nu]}^{>\epsilon}.

Following these notations, the result of the proposition reads

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{2}\bigg(\sqrt{\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{+}}\Pi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\big]\mu_{\mathcal{O}},~\sqrt{\Sigma[\nu]^{+}}\Pi_{\Sigma[\nu]}^{>\epsilon}\nu\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\big(\sqrt{2n}+2\big)\bigg(\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)\cdot\bigg(\alpha\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)^{1/2}\cdot\bigg(2\bigg(\frac{\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}\bigg)^{1/2}+\alpha\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\big(\sqrt{2n}+2\big)\bigg(\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)\cdot\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)^{1/2}\cdot\bigg(\bigg(\frac{\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}}\bigg)^{1/2}+\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)^{1/2},

since
2\leq\alpha
.
In order to simplify this expression, we use the following inequality, valid for
n\geq 0
:

\alpha\big(\sqrt{2n}+2\big)\bigg(\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg)=3(n+1)\big(\sqrt{2n}+2\big)\bigg(\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}^{3}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{3}}\bigg)\leq 8(n+1)^{3/2}\bigg(\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}^{3}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{3}}\bigg).

Injected in the equation above, we obtain the result.
∎

Remark 5.8
.

The stability of
Step 1
, expressed in Proposition
5.7
, shows that the output distance is of order
\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)^{1/2}
.
The linear dependence in
\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)
has been lost in Proposition
5.6
, because of the non-orthogonality of
\mathcal{O}
.
However, when
\mathcal{O}
comes from an orthogonal representation, we have
\mathbb{V}\big[\|\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\|\big]=0
, and the result of the proposition is a linear bound:

\mathrm{W}_{2}\bigg(\sqrt{\Sigma[\mu_{\mathcal{O}}]^{+}}\Pi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\big]\mu_{\mathcal{O}},~\sqrt{\Sigma[\nu]^{+}}\Pi_{\Sigma[\nu]}^{>\epsilon}\nu\bigg)\leq 8(n+1)^{3/2}\bigg(\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}^{3}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{3}}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg).

5.2
Analysis of Lie-PCA

This section is devoted to the study of Lie PCA,
Step 2
of our algorithm.
As presented in the original paper
[
15
]
, it has been designed to estimate the symmetry group of a manifold
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, based on the observation of a point cloud
X
sampled on it.
As written in Equation (
8
), Lie-PCA is based on the operator
\Lambda\colon\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
defined as

\Lambda(A)=\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}\widehat{\Pi}\big[\mathrm{N}_{x_{i}}X\big]\cdot A\cdot\Pi\big[\langle x_{i}\rangle\big],

where
X=\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
is the input point cloud, and the
\widehat{\Pi}\big[\mathrm{N}_{x_{i}}X\big]
’s are
n\times n
matrices, understood as estimators of the projection matrices on the normal spaces of the underlying manifold
\mathcal{O}
.
In order to study the operator
\Lambda
through the lenses of the Wasserstein distance, we must define a version of it adapted to measures.
Given a measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with support satisfying
0\notin\mathrm{supp}(\mu)
, and any map
\mathrm{N}[\mu]\colon\mathrm{supp}(\mu)\rightarrow\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, we define the operator
\Lambda[\mu,\mathrm{N}[\mu]]\colon\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
as

\Lambda[\mu,\mathrm{N}[\mu]](A)=\int\mathrm{N}[\mu](x)\cdot A\cdot\Pi\big[\langle x\rangle\big]\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

In particular, if
\mu=\mu_{\mathcal{O}}
is the uniform measure on the orbit
\mathcal{O}
, and
\mathrm{N}[\mathcal{O}]\colon\mathcal{O}\rightarrow\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
is the map such that
\mathrm{N}[\mathcal{O}](x)=\Pi\big[\mathrm{N}_{x}\mathcal{O}\big]
, where
\mathrm{N}_{x}\mathcal{O}
is the normal space of
\mathcal{O}
at
x
, then
\Lambda[\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\mathrm{N}[\mathcal{O}]]
is to be understood as the
ideal
Lie-PCA operator, that we wish to estimate.
We denote it
\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}
:

\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}(A)=\int\Pi\big[\mathrm{N}_{x}\mathcal{O}\big]\cdot A\cdot\Pi\big[\langle x\rangle\big]\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{O}}(x).

(30)

As we show below, its kernel is
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
, hence it allows us to estimate the symmetry group.

We start by studying the ideal Lie-PCA operator
\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}
in Section
5.2.1
.
Next, we quantify in Section
5.2.2
the stability of
\Lambda
under small variations of
X
.
Conjugated with stability results for the estimation of normal spaces, that we gather in Section
5.2.3
, we obtain the main result of this section, Proposition
5.15
, that guarantees the accuracy of Lie-PCA in practice.

5.2.1 Consistency of Lie-PCA

We first consider the idealized setting where, instead of a point cloud
X
, we have access to the whole orbit
\mathcal{O}
, as well as
\mu_{\mathcal{O}}
, the uniform measure on it, the normal spaces
\mathrm{N}_{x}\mathcal{O}
, and the ideal Lie-PCA operator
\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}
, defined in Equation (
30
).
We show in the following proposition that its kernel is
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
, and study its eigenvalues, subsequently enabling us to obtain precise estimation results.
From a statistical point of view, this result is a quantification of the confidence one has using Lie-PCA.
We stress that such a study has already been initiated in the original paper, in the context of a point cloud observation
[
15
, Sec. 4]
.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be the orbit of a representation of a compact Lie group
G
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}
the ideal Lie-PCA operator.
Then its kernel is equal
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
.
Moreover,
\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}
is equivariant with respect to the action of
G
on
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
by conjugation.
Last, when
\mathcal{O}
is the sphere
S^{n-1}
, its nonzero eigenvalues are exactly
\delta_{n}
and
\delta^{\prime}_{n}
where

\delta_{n}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n(n(n+1)-2)}~~~~~~\mathrm{and}~~~~~~\delta^{\prime}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}.

Proof.

In this proof, we let
\Lambda
denote the operator
\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}
, and write
\phi\colon G\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
the representation.
We divide the proof into three steps, following the three statements of the proposition.

First step
(Kernel of
\Lambda
).
As shown in
[
15
, Lemma 2]
, the product
\Pi\big[\mathrm{N}_{x}\mathcal{O}\big]\cdot A\cdot\Pi\big[\langle x\rangle\big]
is equal to the projection
\Pi\big[(S_{x}\mathcal{O})^{\bot}\big](A)
, where
S_{x}\mathcal{O}=\{A\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\mid Ax\in\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{O}\}
.
Combined with the definition of
\Lambda
in Equation (
30
), it follows that

\Lambda(A)=\int\Pi\big[(S_{x}\mathcal{O})^{\bot}\big](A)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{O}}(x).

(31)

Besides, we observe that, by the linearity of the integral,

\displaystyle\langle\Lambda(A),A\rangle=\int\big\langle\Pi\big[(S_{x}\mathcal{O})^{\bot}\big](A),A\big\rangle\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{O}}(x)=\int\big\|\Pi\big[(S_{x}\mathcal{O})^{\bot}\big](A)\big\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{O}}(x).

(32)

We now prove that the kernel of
\Lambda
is
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
.
Let
A
be an eigenvector of
\Lambda
with eigenvalue
\lambda
.
In particular,
\langle\Lambda(A),A\rangle=\lambda\|A\|^{2}
.
Injected in Equation (
32
), we get that
\lambda=0
if and only if
A
is orthogonal to
(S_{x}\mathcal{O})^{\bot}
for each
x\in\mathcal{O}
, that is, if and only if
A\in\bigcap_{x\in\mathcal{O}}S_{x}\mathcal{O}
.
Moreover, we have seen in Equation (
2
) that
\bigcap_{x\in\mathcal{O}}S_{x}\mathcal{O}=\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
.
In conclusion, the kernel of
\Lambda
is
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
.

Second step
(Equivariance property).
In order to study
\Lambda
further, we will derive another formulation.
Let
x_{0}\in\mathcal{O}
be a point, fixed until the end of the proof.
Any other
x\in\mathcal{O}
can be written
x=\phi(g)x_{0}
for some
g\in G
.
By a direct inspection of the definition of
S_{x}\mathcal{O}
, we see that

\displaystyle(S_{x}\mathcal{O})^{\bot}

\displaystyle=\phi(g)\cdot(S_{x_{0}}\mathcal{O})^{\bot}\cdot\phi(g)^{-1}

\displaystyle\mathrm{and}~~~~~\Pi\big[(S_{x}\mathcal{O})^{\bot}\big](A)

\displaystyle=\phi(g)\cdot\Pi\big[(S_{x_{0}}\mathcal{O})^{\bot}\big]\bigg(\phi(g)^{-1}A\phi(g)\bigg)\cdot\phi(g)^{-1}.

(33)

Inserting Equation (
5.2.1
) into Equation (
31
) yields the expression:

\displaystyle\Lambda(A)

\displaystyle=\int_{G}\phi(g)\cdot\Pi\big[(S_{x_{0}}\mathcal{O})^{\bot}\big]\bigg(\phi(g)^{-1}A\phi(g)\bigg)\cdot\phi(g)^{-1}\mathrm{d}(g),

(34)

with integral taken over the Haar measure on
G
.
From this formula, we get that the relation

\displaystyle\phi(g)\Lambda(A)\phi(g)^{-1}=\Lambda\bigg(\phi(g)A\phi(g)^{-1}\bigg)

holds for all
A\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
and
g\in G
.
In other words,
\Lambda
is an equivariant operator from
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
to itself, with respect to the action by conjugation of
G
.
Consequently, by Schur’s lemma, there exists a decomposition
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{p}V_{j}
of this representation into irreps such that for all
j\in[1\dots p]
,
\Lambda
restricted to
V_{j}
is a homothety.
Their ratios are the eigenvalues of
\Lambda
.

Next, let
(B_{i})_{i=1}^{n-d}
denote an orthonormal basis of
(S_{x_{0}}\mathcal{O})^{\bot}
.
It has dimension
n-d
since, as we see from its definition, its complement has dimension
d+n(n-1)
.
For any
A\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, the orthogonal projection on
(S_{x_{0}}\mathcal{O})^{\bot}
can be written as

\Pi\big[(S_{x_{0}}\mathcal{O})^{\bot}\big](A)=\sum_{i=1}^{n-d}\langle A,B_{i}\rangle B_{i}.

Injected into Equation (
34
), and manipulating the matrix inner product, we see that

\displaystyle\langle\Lambda(A),A\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n-d}\int\langle A,\phi(g)B_{i}\phi(g)^{-1}\rangle^{2}\mathrm{d}(g).

These integrals are matrix coefficients, thus, Schur orthogonality relations allow to decompose

\displaystyle\int\langle A,\phi(g)B_{i}\phi(g)^{-1}\rangle^{2}\mathrm{d}(g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\int\langle\Pi\big[V_{j}\big](A),\phi(g)\Pi\big[V_{j}\big](B_{i})\phi(g)^{-1}\rangle^{2}\mathrm{d}(g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\|\Pi\big[V_{j}\big](A)\|^{2}\|\Pi\big[V_{j}\big](B_{i})\|^{2}\dim(V_{j})^{-1}.

Taking the sum over the basis of
(S_{x_{0}}\mathcal{O})^{\bot}
yields

\displaystyle\langle\Lambda(A),A\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n-d}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\|\Pi\big[V_{j}\big](A)\|^{2}\|\Pi\big[V_{j}\big](B_{i})\|^{2}\dim(V_{j})^{-1}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\|\Pi\big[V_{j}\big](A)\|^{2}\beta_{j}\dim(V_{j})^{-1},

where
\beta_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{n-d}\|\Pi\big[V_{j}\big](B_{i})\|^{2}
.
We conclude that the eigenvalues of
\Lambda
are exactly the

\beta_{j}\dim(V_{j})^{-1},~j\in[1\dots p].

(35)

Third step
(Case
\mathcal{O}=S^{n-1}
).
We now study the particular case where
\mathcal{O}
is the unit sphere of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
As we see from its definition in Equation (
31
), the Lie-PCA operator
\Lambda
depends only on
\mathcal{O}
, and not on
G
.
Thus, without loss of generality, we can suppose that
G
is
\mathrm{SO}(n)
, acting canonically on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
In this case, the action by conjugation on
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
is well understood: it decomposes into three invariant subspaces, namely the space of the skew-symmetric matrices, the space generated by the identity matrix
I
, and the space of the symmetric matrices of trace zero.
We will denote this decomposition as
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})=\mathfrak{so}(n)\oplus V\oplus W
.
As shown in the previous step,
\Lambda
is an equivariant operator, hence its eigenspaces are precisely given by these three spaces.

The skew-symmetric matrices correspond to the eigenvalue
0
.
Indeed, the symmetry group of
S^{n-1}
is
\mathrm{SO}(n)
.
Hence its Lie algebra,
\mathfrak{so}(n)
, forms the kernel of
\Lambda
, as proved in the first step.

To continue, let us observe that, for any
x_{0}\in S^{n-1}
, the normal space
\mathrm{N}_{x_{0}}\mathcal{O}
is generated by
x_{0}
.
Consequently, the subspace
(S_{x_{0}}\mathcal{O})^{\bot}
, of dimension
1=n-(n-1)
, is generated by the projection matrix
\Pi\big[\langle x_{0}\rangle\big]
.
By a direct computation, one sees that the projection of
\Pi\big[\langle x_{0}\rangle\big]
on
V
is equal to
I/n
.
Applying Equation (
35
), we obtain that
V
is associated with the eigenvalue

\frac{\|I/n\|^{2}}{\dim(V)}=\frac{1}{n}.

Last, we turn to
W
, the symmetric matrices of trace zero.
The projection of
\Pi\big[\langle x_{0}\rangle\big]
on it is equal to
\Pi\big[\langle x_{0}\rangle\big]-I/n
.
Just as before, we deduce that
W
is associated with the eigenvalue

\frac{\|\Pi\big[\langle x_{0}\rangle\big]-I/n\|^{2}}{\dim(W)}=\frac{1-1/n}{n(n+1)/2-1},

which is equal to the quantity
\delta_{n}
in the statement of the proposition.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

In Proposition
5.9
, it is not possible to obtain an ‘universal’ lower bound on the nonzero eigenvalues of
\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}
, i.e., a bound that would be independent of
\mathcal{O}
, even with
n
or
G
being fixed.
To illustrate this, let us consider the representation
\phi\colon\mathrm{SO}(2)\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{4}(\mathbb{R})
with distinct positive weights
(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})
.
Let
\epsilon>0
,
x_{0}=(1,0,\epsilon,0)
a point, and
\mathcal{O}
its orbit.
Explicitly,

\mathcal{O}=\big\{\big(\cos(\omega_{1}\theta),\sin(\omega_{1}\theta),\epsilon\cos(\omega_{2}\theta),\epsilon\sin(\omega_{2}\theta)\big)\mid\theta\in\mathrm{SO}(2)\big\}.

As we compute in Appendix
A.2.3
, the Lie-PCA operator
\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}
admits three nonzero eigenvalues:

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2(1+\epsilon^{2})}\frac{w_{1}^{2}+w_{2}^{2}}{w_{1}^{2}+(\epsilon w_{2})^{2}},~~~~~\frac{1}{8}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{1+\epsilon^{2}}\frac{(w_{1}-\epsilon^{2}w_{2})^{2}}{w_{1}^{2}+(\epsilon w_{2})^{2}}\bigg),~~~~~\frac{1}{8}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{1+\epsilon^{2}}\frac{(w_{1}+\epsilon^{2}w_{2})^{2}}{w_{1}^{2}+(\epsilon w_{2})^{2}}\bigg).

When
\epsilon
tends to zero, this first eigenvalue goes to zero.
This shows that there is no lower bound on the nonzero eigenvalues.
Nonetheless, an interesting observation can be made in the case where
\epsilon=1
.
This corresponds to a homogeneous orbit, that is, an orbit whose covariance matrix is a homothety, as evoked in Section
5.1.1
.
In this case, the eigenvalues above become

\frac{1}{4},~~~~~\frac{1}{8}\bigg(1+\frac{(w_{1}-w_{2})^{2}}{2(w_{1}^{2}+w_{2}^{2})}\bigg)~~~~~\frac{1}{8}\bigg(1+\frac{(w_{1}+w_{2})^{2}}{2(w_{1}^{2}+w_{2}^{2})}\bigg),

all belonging to the interval
[1/8,1/4]
.
More generally, we have observed experimentally that, for a homogeneous orbit
\mathcal{O}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the nonzero eigenvalues of
\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}
belong to
[1/n^{2},1/n]
, leaving open the possibility of an ‘universal’ lower bound in this case.

5.2.2 Stability of Lie-PCA

We now study the stability of the Lie-PCA operator, with respect to small variations in Wasserstein distance.
Let
\mu
and
\nu
be two measures on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\mathrm{N}[\mu]\colon\mathrm{supp}(\mu)\rightarrow\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathrm{N}[\nu]\colon\mathrm{supp}(\nu)\rightarrow\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
two maps, and consider the corresponding operators
\Lambda[\mu,\mathrm{N}[\mu]]
and
\Lambda[\nu,\mathrm{N}[\nu]]
.
We wish to give an upper bound on the operator norm
\|\Lambda[\mu,\mathrm{N}[\mu]]-\Lambda[\nu,\mathrm{N}[\nu]]\|_{\mathrm{op}}
.
As can be expected, this bound will depend on the Wasserstein distance between
\mu
and
\nu
, but also between
\mathrm{N}[\mu]
and
\mathrm{N}[\nu]
.
In order to obtain explicit bounds, we choose an optimal transport plan
\pi
for the Wasserstein distance
\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)
, and define

\Omega=\int\|\mathrm{N}[\mu](x)-\mathrm{N}[\nu](y)\|\mathrm{d}\pi(x,y).

(36)

This quantity is a measure of the distance between the estimations of normal spaces.
We prove in this section a lemma that formulates the stability of the Lie-PCA operator in terms of
\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)
and
\Omega
.
In the next section, we will show how
\Omega
can be bounded by
\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)
, once a specific normal space estimator has been chosen, and provided regularity conditions on
\mu
.

Lemma 5.11
.

If the essential suprema
\sup(\|\mathrm{N}[\mu]\|)
and
\sup(\|\mu\|^{-1})
are finite, then it holds

\|\Lambda[\mu,\mathrm{N}[\mu]]-\Lambda[\nu,\mathrm{N}[\nu]]\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 2\sup(\|\mathrm{N}[\mu]\|)\sup(\|\mu\|^{-1})\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)+\Omega.

Proof.

By definition of the operator norm, we have

\displaystyle\|\Lambda[\mu,\mathrm{N}[\mu]]-\Lambda[\nu,\mathrm{N}[\nu]]\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\sup\|\Lambda[\mu,\mathrm{N}[\mu]](A)-\Lambda[\nu,\mathrm{N}[\nu]](A)\|

where the supremum is taken over all matrices
A
such that
\|A\|=1
. Let
A
be such a matrix.
We first use the inequality

\displaystyle\|\Lambda[\mu,\mathrm{N}[\mu]](A)-\Lambda[\nu,\mathrm{N}[\nu]]\|

\displaystyle\leq\int\big\|N[\mu](x)\cdot A\cdot\Pi\big[\langle x\rangle\big]-N[\nu](y)\cdot A\cdot\Pi\big[\langle y\rangle\big]\big\|\mathrm{d}\pi(x,y).

The term under the integral is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\underbrace{\|\mathrm{N}[\mu](x)\cdot A\cdot(\Pi\big[\langle x\rangle\big]-\Pi\big[\langle y\rangle\big])\|}_{(\mathrm{a})}+\underbrace{\|(\mathrm{N}[\mu](x)-\mathrm{N}[\nu](y))\cdot A\cdot\Pi\big[\langle y\rangle\big]\|}_{\mathrm{(b)}}.

(37)

In order to bound Term
(a)
, we use the submultiplicativity of the Frobenius norm:

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{N}[\mu](x)\cdot A\cdot(\Pi\big[\langle x\rangle\big]-\Pi\big[\langle y\rangle\big])\|\leq\|\mathrm{N}[\mu](x)\|\cdot\|A\|\cdot\|\Pi\big[\langle x\rangle\big]-\Pi\big[\langle y\rangle\big]\|.

(38)

By writing
\Pi\big[\langle x\rangle\big]=(x/\|x\|)\cdot(x/\|x\|)^{\top}
and
\Pi\big[\langle y\rangle\big]=(y/\|y\|)\cdot(y/\|y\|)^{\top}
, we see that Lemma
5.2
, stated in Section
5.1.1
, yields
\|\Pi\big[\langle x\rangle\big]-\Pi\big[\langle y\rangle\big]\|\leq\|x/\|x\|-y/\|y\|\|
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\bigg\|\frac{x}{\|x\|}-\frac{y}{\|y\|}\bigg\|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\|x\|\|y\|}\bigg\|\|y\|x-\|x\|y\bigg\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\|x\|\|y\|}\bigg(\bigg\|\|y\|x-\|y\|y\bigg\|+\bigg\|\|y\|y-\|x\|y\bigg\|\bigg)=\frac{2}{\|x\|}\|x-y\|.

We eventually deduce from Equation (
38
) that Term
(a)
is upper bounded by

\sup(\|\mathrm{N}[\mu]\|)\cdot 1\cdot 2\sup(\|\mu\|^{-1})\|x-y\|
.
Consequently, its integral over the transport plan
\pi
is bounded by

2\sup(\|\mathrm{N}[\mu]\|)\sup(\|\mu\|^{-1})\int\|x-y\|\mathrm{d}\pi(x,y)\leq 2\sup(\|\mathrm{N}[\mu]\|)\sup(\|\mu\|^{-1})\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu),

(39)

where we applied Jensen’s inequality.
To finish the proof, we turn to Term
(b)
. The projection matrix
\Pi\big[\langle y\rangle\big]
has Frobenius norm equal to
1
.
Thus, the integral of Term
(b)
is upper bounded by
\int\|(\mathrm{N}[\mu](x)-\mathrm{N}[\nu](y))\|\mathrm{d}\pi(x,y)
, that is,
\Omega
.
Injecting this inequality and Equation (
39
) in Equation (
37
) yields the result.
∎

5.2.3 Stability of normal space estimation

In order to turn Lemma
5.11
into a result applicable in practice, we need to choose a method for estimating normal spaces, and bound the term
\Omega
defined in Equation (
36
).
For any
x\in\mathcal{O}
, we denote by
\mathrm{N}_{x}\mathcal{O}
the normal space of
\mathcal{O}
at
x
.
Besides, let
X=\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
denote the observed point cloud.
As discussed in Section
3.3.2
, for any integer
i\in[0\dots N]
, we chose as an estimator of
\Pi\big[\mathrm{N}_{x_{i}}\mathcal{O}\big]
the matrix

\widehat{\Pi}\big[\mathrm{N}_{x_{i}}X\big]=I-\Pi_{x_{i}}^{l,r}[X],

(40)

where
\Pi_{x_{i}}^{l,r}[X]
is the projection matrix on any
l
top eigenvectors of the
local covariance matrix

\Sigma_{x_{i}}^{r}[X]
centered at
x_{i}
and at scale
r
, itself defined as

\Sigma_{x_{i}}^{r}[X]=\frac{1}{|Y|}\sum_{y\in Y}(y-x_{i})(y-x_{i})^{\top},

where
Y=\{y\in X\mid\|y-x_{i}\|\leq r\}
, the set input points at distance at most
r
from
x_{i}
.
These notions directly translate in the measure-theoretical framework: if
\mu
is a measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we define its
local covariance matrix
centered at
x
at scale
r
as

\Sigma_{x}^{r}[\mu]=\int_{\mathcal{B}(x,r)}(y-x)(y-x)^{\top}\frac{d\mu(x)}{\mu(\mathcal{B}(x,r))},

where
\mathcal{B}(x,r)
is the closed ball of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
centered at
x
and of radius
r
.
Just as in the discrete case, we denote by
\Pi_{x}^{l,r}[\mu]
the projection matrix on any
l
top eigenvectors of
\Sigma_{x}^{r}[\mu]
.

It is direct to see that, if
\mu
is the empirical measure on the finite point cloud
X
, then
\Sigma^{r}_{x}[X]
and
\Sigma^{r}_{x}[\mu]
coincide for every
x\in X
.
That is to say, one is able to study discrete covariance matrices via their measure-theoretic formulations.
Besides, if
\mu=\mu_{\mathcal{M}}
is a ‘regular enough’ measure on a submanifold
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
of dimension
l
, and
x\in\mathcal{M}
, then it is known that
(l+2)/r^{2}\cdot\Sigma_{x}^{r}[\mu_{\mathcal{M}}]
tends to
\Pi\big[\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}\big]
when
r
goes to zero, with
\Pi\big[\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}\big]
the projection matrix on the tangent space.
In order to study how accurate this estimation is, it is natural to consider the following decomposition, which can be seen as a bias-variance trade-off.
For any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\bigg\|\frac{1}{l+2}\Pi\big[\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}\big]-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Sigma_{y}^{r}[\nu]\bigg\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\bigg\|\frac{1}{l+2}\Pi\big[\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}\big]-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Sigma_{x}^{r}[\mu_{\mathcal{M}}]\bigg\|_{\mathrm{F}}}_{\mathrm{consistency}}+\underbrace{\bigg\|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Sigma_{x}^{r}[\mu_{\mathcal{M}}]-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Sigma_{y}^{r}[\mu_{\mathcal{M}}]\bigg\|_{\mathrm{F}}}_{\mathrm{spatial~stability}}+\underbrace{\bigg\|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Sigma_{y}^{r}[\mu_{\mathcal{M}}]-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Sigma_{y}^{r}[\nu]\bigg\|_{\mathrm{F}}}_{\mathrm{measure~stability}}.

In order to bound on these quantities, one needs additional assumptions, in particular regarding the geometry of
\mathcal{M}
.
We state below a result that involves the
reach
of
\mathcal{M}
, denoted
\mathrm{reach}(\mathcal{M})
.
It is a quantity associated to submanifolds of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and is closely connected to the notion of curvature.
We refer the reader to
[
31
]
for an exposition of this notion.

Lemma 5.12

(
[
85
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a compact
\mathcal{C}^{2}
-submanifold of dimension
l
and
\mu_{\mathcal{M}}
a measure, absolutely continuous with respect to the
l
-dimensional Hausdorff measure restricted to
\mathcal{M}
, and such that its density
f
is
L
-Lipschitz (with respect to the geodesic distance) and bounded by
f_{\mathrm{min}},f_{\mathrm{max}}>0
. Moreover, let
\nu
be any measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
p\geq 1
a real number, and choose
x\in\mathcal{M}
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
r>0
such that

\|x-y\|\leq\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{p}(\mu,\nu)}{r^{l-1}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}~~~~~~\mathrm{and}~~~~~~~4\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{p}(\mu,\nu)}{V_{l}f_{\mathrm{min}}(23/24)^{l}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{l+1}}\leq r<\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{reach}(\mathcal{M}).

where
V_{l}
is the volume of the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{l}
.
Then it holds that

\displaystyle\bigg\|\Pi\big[\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}\big]-\frac{l+2}{r^{2}}\Sigma_{y}^{r}[\nu]\bigg\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq cr+c^{\prime}\|x-y\|+c^{\prime\prime}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{p}(\mu,\nu)}{r^{l+1}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where the constants are given in Equations (
41
), (
42
) and (
43
).
Moreover, if the left-hand term of the inequality is lower than
1/2
, then

\displaystyle\big\|\Pi\big[\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}\big]-\Pi_{y}^{l,r}[\nu]\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\sqrt{2}\bigg(cr+c^{\prime}\|x-y\|+c^{\prime\prime}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{p}(\mu,\nu)}{r^{l+1}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg).

Proof.

The lemma is a consequence of the three following bounds, whose proofs are given in
[
85
]
under the names Proposition 4.1, Lemma 4.5 and Lemma 4.7.
The first states that, for any
r<\mathrm{reach}(\mathcal{M})/2
and
x\in\mathcal{M}
, one has

\displaystyle\bigg\|\frac{l+2}{r^{2}}\Sigma_{x}^{r}[\mu_{\mathcal{M}}]-\Pi\big[\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{O}\big]\bigg\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq cr

\displaystyle\mathrm{with}~~~~~

\displaystyle c=\frac{6(l+2)}{\mathrm{reach}(M)}+\frac{l+2}{f_{\mathrm{min}}}\bigg(\frac{24}{23}\bigg)^{l}\bigg(20L\bigg(\frac{5}{4}\bigg)^{l}+\frac{f_{\mathrm{max}}l}{2\mathrm{reach}(\mathcal{M})}\bigg(5+2^{l}+2\bigg(\frac{5}{2}\bigg)^{l}\bigg)\bigg).

(41)

Secondly, for
r<\mathrm{reach}(\mathcal{M})/2
,
x\in\mathcal{M}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\|x-y\|<r/4
, it holds that

\bigg\|\frac{l+2}{r^{2}}\Sigma_{x}^{r}[\mu_{\mathcal{M}}]-\frac{l+2}{r^{2}}\Sigma_{y}^{r}[\mu_{\mathcal{M}}]\bigg\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq c^{\prime}\|x-y\|~~~~\mathrm{with}~~~~c^{\prime}=l2^{l+1}\bigg(1+4\frac{5^{l-1}}{3^{l}}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{30}{23}\bigg)^{l}\frac{f_{\mathrm{max}}}{f_{\mathrm{min}}}.

(42)

Last, for any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
at distance at most
(\mathrm{W}_{p}(\mu,\nu)/r^{l-1})^{\frac{1}{2}}
from
\mathrm{supp}(\mu)
, and for
r
such that
\mathrm{W}_{p}(\mu,\nu)\leq\min(1,V_{l}f_{\mathrm{min}}(23/24)^{l})(r/4)^{l+1}
and
\leq r<\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{reach}(\mathcal{M})
, one has

\displaystyle\bigg\|\frac{l+2}{r^{2}}\Sigma_{y}[\mu]-\frac{l+2}{r^{2}}\Sigma_{y}[\nu]\bigg\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq c^{\prime\prime}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{p}(\mu,\nu)}{r^{l+1}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{with}~~~~~c^{\prime\prime}=l2^{l+1}(l+2)\bigg(2^{l-1}+2\frac{12\cdot 5^{l-1}+1}{3^{l}}+2^{l+3}\bigg(\bigg(\frac{3}{2}\bigg)^{l-1}+1\bigg)\bigg),

(43)

and where
V_{l}
denotes the volume of the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{l}
.
Summing these inequalities gives the bound on
\|\Pi\big[\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}\big]-\frac{l+2}{r^{2}}\Sigma_{y}^{r}[\nu]\|_{\mathrm{F}}
.
The second statement is directly obtained by application of Davis-Kahan theorem (Lemma
5.3
), and observing that the
l^{\mathrm{th}}
eigengap of
\Pi\big[\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}\big]
is
1
.
∎

Based on this lemma, we deduce a bound on the quantity
\Omega
introduced in Equation (
36
).
In this context, the manifold
M
is the orbit
\mathcal{O}
, and the measure
\mu_{M}
is the uniform measure on
\mathcal{O}
, permitting a simplification of the constants
c
,
c^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime\prime}
.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be an orbit of representation of a compact Lie group in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
l
its dimension, and
\mu_{\mathcal{O}}
the uniform measure on it.
Let also
\nu
be any measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\pi
an optimal transport plan for
\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)
.
Let
r>0
be such that

\big(6\rho\big)^{2}\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)^{\frac{1}{l+1}}\leq r\leq\big(6\rho\big)^{-1}~~~~\mathrm{where}~~~~~\rho=\bigg(16l(l+2)6^{l}\bigg)\frac{\max(\mathrm{vol}(\mathcal{O}),\mathrm{vol}(\mathcal{O})^{-1})}{\min(1,\mathrm{reach}(\mathcal{O}))}.

Then it holds that

\int\big\|\Pi\big[\mathrm{N}_{x}\mathcal{O}\big]-\big(I-\Pi_{y}^{r,l}[\nu]\big)\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}\mathrm{d}\pi(x,y)\leq\sqrt{2}\rho\bigg(r+3\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{r^{l+1}}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg).

Proof.

We first observe that, as a consequence of the relation
I=\Pi\big[\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{O}\big]+\Pi\big[\mathrm{N}_{x}\mathcal{O}\big]
, the integral is equal to
\int\|\Pi\big[\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{O}\big]-\Pi_{y}^{r,l}[\nu]\|\mathrm{d}\pi(x,y)
.
We split it into the set
\Delta
and its complement
\Delta^{\complement}
, where we define
\Delta=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\|x-y\|\leq\alpha\}
, and
\alpha=(\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)/r^{l-1})^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
As it will be clearer later in the proof, the constant
\alpha
has been chosen so as to calibrate the contributions of
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\complement}
.
On the set
\Delta^{\complement}
, Markov’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\pi\big(\Delta^{\complement}\big)

\displaystyle=\pi\big(\big\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\|x-y\|^{2}\geq\alpha^{2}\big\}\big)\leq\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{\alpha}\bigg)^{2}.

Since
(\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)/\alpha)^{2}=\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)r^{l-1}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{\Delta^{\complement}}\big\|\Pi\big[\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{O}\big]-\Pi_{y}^{r,l}[\nu]\|_{\mathrm{F}}\mathrm{d}\pi(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\sup\big\|\Pi\big[\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{O}\big]-\Pi_{y}^{r,l}[\nu]\|_{\mathrm{F}}\cdot\pi(\Delta^{\complement})

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{l}\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)r^{l-1}.

(44)

We now turn to the set
\Delta
.
For
(x,y)\in\Delta
, the hypotheses of Lemma
5.12
are satisfied, hence

\displaystyle\bigg\|\Pi\big[\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{O}\big]-\frac{l+2}{r^{2}}\Sigma_{y}^{r}[\nu]\bigg\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq cr+c^{\prime}\|x-y\|+c^{\prime\prime}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{r^{l+1}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(45)

Moreover, in our context, we have
L=0
and
f_{\mathrm{min}}=f_{\mathrm{min}}=\mathrm{vol}(\mathcal{O})^{-1}
, where the volume of
\mathcal{O}
refers to its
l
-dimensional Hausdorff measure. Simple manipulations show that we have

c\leq\max(\mathrm{vol}(\mathcal{O}),\mathrm{vol}(\mathcal{O})^{-1})\frac{16l(l+2)}{\mathrm{reach}(\mathcal{O})}\bigg(\frac{60}{23}\bigg)^{l},~~~~~c^{\prime}\leq 4l\bigg(\frac{100}{23}\bigg)^{l}~~~~~\mathrm{and}~~~~~c^{\prime\prime}\leq 16l(l+2)6^{l}.

In particular, they are all lower than

\rho=\bigg(16l(l+2)6^{l}\bigg)\frac{\max(\mathrm{vol}(\mathcal{O}),\mathrm{vol}(\mathcal{O})^{-1})}{\min(1,\mathrm{reach}(\mathcal{O}))}.

Thus, we deduce from Equation (
45
) that

\displaystyle\bigg\|\Pi\big[\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{O}\big]-\frac{l+2}{r^{2}}\Sigma_{y}^{r}[\nu]\bigg\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\rho r+\rho\|x-y\|+\rho\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{r^{l+1}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\frac{1}{6}+\frac{1}{6}+\frac{1}{6}=\frac{1}{2}.

The fact that each term bounded by
1/6
comes from the hypothesis of the lemma regarding
r
.
As a consequence, we can apply the second point of Lemma
5.12
and get

\displaystyle\bigg\|\Pi\big[\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{O}\big]-\Pi_{y}^{r,l}[\nu]\bigg\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\rho\bigg(r+\|x-y\|+\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{r^{l+1}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg).

Integrating this expression over
\Delta
yields

\displaystyle\int_{\Delta}\big\|\Pi\big[\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{O}\big]-\Pi_{y}^{r,l}[\nu]\|_{\mathrm{F}}\mathrm{d}\pi(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\rho\bigg(r+\int_{\Delta}\|x-y\|\mathrm{d}\pi(x,y)+\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{r^{l+1}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\rho\bigg(r+\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)+\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{r^{l+1}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg),

where we used Jensen’s inequality on the second line.
Combined with Equation (
5.2.3
), we get a bound on the full integral:

\displaystyle\int\big\|\Pi\big[\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathcal{O}\big]-\Pi_{y}^{r,l}[\nu]\|_{\mathrm{F}}\mathrm{d}\pi\leq 2\sqrt{l}\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)r^{l-1}+\sqrt{2}\rho\bigg(r+\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)+\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{r^{l+1}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg).

Besides, using the hypothesis on
r
, we see that

2\sqrt{l}\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)r^{l-1}\leq\sqrt{2}\rho\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{r^{l+1}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}~~~~~\mathrm{and}~~~~~\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)\leq\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)}{r^{l+1}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}},

and the result of the lemma follows.
∎

Remark 5.14
.

The inequality of Lemma
5.13
can be seen as a bias-variance trade-off, the former being
r
, and the latter
(\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\nu)/r^{l+1})^{1/2}
.
In other words, the estimation of tangent and normal spaces is more accurate when
r
is small but becomes less stable with respect to variations.
Besides, a word should be said about the exponent
1/2
on the Wasserstein distance.
In the general context of localization of measures, it can be shown that this exponent is optimal
[
85
, Remark 4.9]
.
However, when it comes to the estimation of tangent spaces via local PCA, we do not know whether it can be improved.
We stress that any improvement of the bound in Lemma
5.12
would result in a direct improvement of Lemma
5.13
, and other results to come.

A direct combination of Lemmas
5.11
and
5.13
yields our main result regarding Lie-PCA.

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be an orbit of representation a compact Lie group in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
l
its dimension, and its
\mu_{\mathcal{O}}
the uniform measure.
Let also
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a finite point cloud and
\mu_{X}
be its empirical measure.
We consider the ideal Lie-PCA operator
\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}
, and the operator
\Lambda
computed from
X
using the estimators of normal spaces defined in Equation (
40
).
Let
r>0
and
\rho
be such as in Lemma
5.13
.
Then it holds that

\|\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}-\Lambda\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\sqrt{2}\rho\bigg(r+4\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\mu_{X})}{r^{l+1}}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg).

5.3
Stability of minimization problems

The execution of the algorithm involves a minimization program, either during
Step 3
, formulated in Equation (
9
), or during
Step 3
’, formulated in Equation (
10
).
In both cases, it consists of several minimizations over
\mathrm{O}(n)
, solved in practice with gradient descent.
Studying the accuracy of such algorithms is, in general, a difficult problem to tackle.
Indeed, their outputs depend crucially on the stopping criterion chosen by the user, and are subject to numerical errors.
But most of all, minimization via gradient descent seeks only a
local minimum
.
Instead of diving into the study of these issues, which is out of the scope of this paper, we will consider that the algorithms always return a global minimum.
Our objective with this section is to prove that the global minima of Equations (
9
) and (
10
) are close to the ‘ideal’ object: a Lie algebra that underlies the data.

At the core of this analysis lies the question of rigidity of Lie subalgebras, which we introduce in Section
5.3.1
.
We then derive stability results for
Step 3
and
Step 3
’ in Section
5.3.2
, enabling us to deduce in Section
5.3.3
the consistency of
Step 4
.

5.3.1 Rigidity of Lie subalgebras

Let us recall the context of this paper:
\mathcal{O}
is an orbit of a representation of a compact Lie group in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
X
is a finite point cloud.
We write
\Lambda
for the Lie-PCA operator obtained from
X
and the estimation of its normal spaces via local PCA.
Moreover, the ideal Lie-PCA operator, obtained by replacing
X
with
\mathcal{O}
, is denoted
\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}
.
Let us consider
Step 3
, that we perform with an input compact Lie group denoted
G
, and whose Lie algebra is
\mathfrak{g}
.
We consider the formulation given in Equation (
14
):

\arg\min\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|\Lambda(A_{i})\|^{2}\;\;\;\text{s.t. }(A_{1},\dots,A_{d})\in\mathcal{V}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{so}(n)).

As a first approximation to study this equation, we consider the ideal case
\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}
.
This operator can be diagonalized as
\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\lambda_{j}\Pi\big[V_{j}\big]
, where the eigenspaces are orthogonal and satisfy
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{p}V_{j}
.
As shown in Proposition
5.9
, its kernel is equal to
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
, i.e., the eigenvalue
0
is associated to the eigenspace
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
.
Denoting
\lambda
its smallest nonzero eigenvalue, it holds

\lambda\cdot\Pi\big[\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})^{\bot}\big]~\leq~\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}.

The sum
\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}(A_{i})\|^{2}
is equal to zero precisely when the frame
(A_{1},\dots,A_{d})
is included in
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
.
Moreover, by denoting
\Pi\big[\langle A_{i}\rangle_{i=1}^{d}\big]
the projection on the space they span, we deduce

\lambda\cdot\big\|\Pi\big[\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})^{\bot}\big]\Pi\big[\langle A_{i}\rangle_{i=1}^{d}\big]\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}~\leq~\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}(A_{i})\|^{2}.

(46)

The Frobenius norm
\big\|\Pi\big[\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})^{\bot}\big]\Pi\big[\langle A_{i}\rangle_{i=1}^{d}\big]\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}
can be understood as a sort of distance between the spaces
\Pi\big[\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})^{\bot}\big]
and
\Pi\big[\langle A_{i}\rangle_{i=1}^{d}\big]
, adapted to the case where they potentially are of different dimensions.
Indeed, when their dimensions coincide, we have

\big\|\Pi\big[\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})^{\bot}\big]\Pi\big[\langle A_{i}\rangle_{i=1}^{d}\big]\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}~=~\sum_{i=1}^{d}\big(1-\|\Pi\big[\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})\big](A_{i})\|^{2}\big)~=~\frac{1}{2}\big\|\Pi\big[\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})\big]-\Pi\big[\langle A_{i}\rangle_{i=1}^{d}\big]\big\|^{2},

where we recognize the distance between subspaces defined in Section
2.3.1
(see Equation (
5
)).

Back to the original problem, and as consequence of this discussion, we are compelled to study the minimum of the function

\mathfrak{h}\mapsto\big\|\Pi\big[\mathfrak{s}^{\bot}\big]\Pi\big[\mathfrak{h}\big]\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}~~~~\mathrm{where}~~~~\mathfrak{h}\in\mathcal{G}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n)),

and where
\mathfrak{s}
is a Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{so}(n)
and
\mathcal{G}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))
the Grassmann variety of pushforward Lie algebras of
G
.
We have seen in Section
2.3.2
that the connected components of
\mathcal{G}(G,\mathfrak{so}(n))
are generated by the action of
\mathrm{O}(n)
on it by conjugation, yielding the quotient space
\mathfrak{orb}(G,n)
.
Let us consider one such component, denoting
(B^{1},\dots,B^{p})\in\mathfrak{orb}(G,n)
an element, and
\mathfrak{h}=\langle\mathrm{diag}\big(B^{k}_{j}\big)_{k=1}^{p}\rangle_{i=1}^{d}
the corresponding Lie algebra.
On this component, the function above reads

O\mapsto\big\|\Pi\big[\mathfrak{s}^{\bot}\big]\Pi\big[O\mathfrak{h}O^{\top}\big]\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}~~~~~\mathrm{where}~~~~~O\in\mathrm{O}(n).

If a certain conjugated Lie algebra
O\mathfrak{h}O^{\top}
belongs to
\mathfrak{s}
, then the minimum of this function is zero.
Otherwise, is it positive, and finding a lower bound has already been studied in the context of
rigidity of Lie subalgebras
.
Namely, the
d
-dimensional Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{so}(n)
is called
rigid
if its orbit
\{O\mathfrak{h}O^{\top}\mid O\in\mathrm{O}(n)\}
, seen in the space of
d
-dimensional Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{so}(n)
, admits a neighborhood consisting only of isomorphic Lie subalgebras.
It is, in general, a hard problem to determine which Lie subalgebras are rigid
[
7
]
.
As a positive result, a sufficient condition for rigidity can be defined from the cohomology of
\mathfrak{h}

[
23
]
.
Another instance of the literature where rigidity is involved is that of
switched linear systems
.
In this context, it has been studied the closely related problem of recovering the Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
, based on the observation of a sufficiently close vector space
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathfrak{so}(n)

[
5
,
4
]
.
Our problem is similar.

As it will be clearer in the next section, quantifying the rigidity of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}
reveals to be crucial to state explicit consistency results for our algorithm.
To this end, we define

\Gamma(G,n)=\inf_{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{s}}\big\|\Pi\big[\mathfrak{s}^{\bot}\big]\Pi\big[\mathfrak{h}\big]\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2},

(47)

where the infimum is taken over all the pairs
(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{s})
where
\mathfrak{h}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{so}(n))
and
\mathfrak{s}
is a Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{so}(n)
, pushforward of any compact Lie group, and that does not contain any conjugation
O\mathfrak{h}O^{\top}
for
O\in\mathrm{O}(n)
.
When there are no such pairs, we set
\Gamma(G,n)=+\infty
.

When
G
is
\mathrm{SU}(2)
or
\mathrm{SO}(3)
, this infimum is greater than zero.
This comes from the fact that, being fixed an ambient dimension
n
, only a finite number of non-equivalent representations exist.
If the group is Abelian, however, the infimum may be zero.
In this case, we restrict the definition of
\Gamma(G,n)
to the finite set of representations used in the algorithm (described in Section
4.1.1
).
More precisely, given a positive integer
\omega_{\mathrm{max}}
, we define
\Gamma(G,n,\omega_{\mathrm{max}})
as in Equation (
47
), but imposing the additional assumption that the Lie subalgebras
\mathfrak{h}
and
\mathfrak{s}
are spanned by matrices whose spectra comes from a primitive integral vector of coordinates at most
\omega_{\mathrm{max}}
.
The following lemma gives a lower bound on this quantity.

Lemma 5.16
.

For any compact Lie group
G
, it holds that
\Gamma(G,n,\omega_{\mathrm{max}})\geq 4/(n\omega_{\mathrm{max}}^{2})
.

Proof.

We start with
G=\mathrm{SO}(2)
.
Without loss of generality, we suppose that
n
is even, and define
m=n/2
.
Let
\mathfrak{h}
and
\mathfrak{s}
be as in Equation (
47
).
Let
A
be a skew-symmetric matrix that spans
\mathfrak{h}
, and that we choose of unit norm.
We have

\big\|\Pi\big[\mathfrak{s}^{\bot}\big]\Pi\big[\mathfrak{h}\big]\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}~=~\big\|\Pi\big[\mathfrak{s}^{\bot}\big](A)\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}~=~\inf_{B\in\mathfrak{s}}\big\|B-A\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}.

(48)

Let
B\in\mathfrak{s}
be of unit norm.
The matrices
A
and
B
are skew-symmetric, hence there exists two
m
-tuples of nonnegative real numbers
(\lambda_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
and
(\lambda^{\prime}_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
such that their spectra takes the form

\pm{i\mkern 1.0mu}\lambda_{1}/\delta,~\dots,~\pm{i\mkern 1.0mu}\lambda_{m}/\delta~~~~\mathrm{and}~~~~\pm{i\mkern 1.0mu}\lambda_{1}^{\prime}/\delta^{\prime},~\dots,~\pm{i\mkern 1.0mu}\lambda_{m}^{\prime}/\delta^{\prime}

with
\delta=\sqrt{2\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}^{2}}
,
\delta^{\prime}=\sqrt{2\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\lambda_{i}^{\prime})^{2}}
, with
{i\mkern 1.0mu}
the imaginary unit.
By assumption, the
\lambda_{i}
’s and
\lambda_{i}^{\prime}
’s can be chosen as integers lower or equal to
\omega_{\mathrm{max}}
.
Hoffman-Wielandt inequality reads

\|B-A\|^{2}\geq 2\sum_{i=1}^{m}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\delta}-\frac{\lambda_{i}^{\prime}}{\delta^{\prime}}\bigg)^{2}.

Note that, in Equation (
48
),
B
does not have to be of unit norm.
The polynomial

t\mapsto 2\sum_{i=1}^{m}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\delta}-t\frac{\lambda_{i}^{\prime}}{\delta^{\prime}}\bigg)^{2},

where
t\geq 0
, admits as a minimum the value

2\bigg(1-\bigg(\frac{1}{\delta\delta^{\prime}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{i}^{\prime}\bigg)^{2}\bigg).

Let us suppose that the
m
-tuples are distinct.
That is, there exists
k\in[1\dots m]
such that
\lambda_{k}\neq\lambda_{k}^{\prime}
.
We compute:

\displaystyle 2\bigg(1-\bigg(\frac{1}{\delta\delta^{\prime}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{i}^{\prime}\bigg)^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle=2\bigg(\bigg(\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}^{2}\bigg)^{2}-\bigg(\frac{1}{\delta\delta^{\prime}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{i}^{\prime}\bigg)^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle=2\bigg(\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}^{2}+\frac{1}{\delta\delta^{\prime}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{i}^{\prime}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}^{2}-\bigg(\frac{1}{\delta\delta^{\prime}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{i}^{\prime}\bigg)

\displaystyle=2\cdot 2\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\delta}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\delta}-\frac{\lambda_{i}^{\prime}}{\delta^{\prime}}\bigg)\geq 4\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\delta}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\delta}-\frac{\lambda_{k}^{\prime}}{\delta^{\prime}}\bigg).

Using that
|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}|\geq 1
and
\delta,\delta^{\prime}\leq\sqrt{2m\omega_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}
, we finally obtain

2\bigg(1-\bigg(\frac{1}{\delta\delta^{\prime}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{i}^{\prime}\bigg)^{2}\bigg)\geq 4\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2m\omega_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2m\omega_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}}\geq\frac{2}{m\omega_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}=\frac{4}{n\omega_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}.

We deduce the claim of the lemma.
More generally, when
G
is a compact Lie group, the bound still holds, since
\big\|\Pi\big[\mathfrak{s}^{\bot}\big]\Pi\big[\mathfrak{h}\big]\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}
is lower bounded by
\big\|\Pi\big[\mathfrak{s}^{\bot}\big](A)\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}
for any unit
A\in\mathfrak{h}
.
∎

5.3.2 Minimization of Equation (
9
)

We now deduce a stability result for
Step 3
.
As a direct consequence of the rigidity of Lie subalgebras, we obtain conditions under which Equation (
9
) is minimized precisely for an element of
\mathfrak{orb}(G,n)
that yields the underlying subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
.
The minimizer
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
, however, may not be close to
\mathfrak{h}
.
This latter problem, known as
stability
of Lie subalgebras, is known to be more subtle than its rigidity equivalent
[
23
, Remark 5.13]
.
We circumvent this issue by working with the quantity
\Gamma(G,n,\omega_{\mathrm{max}})
.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be an orbit of a compact Lie group in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
X
a finite point cloud, and
G
a compact Lie group of dimension
d
.
Let
\lambda
be the smallest nonzero eigenvalue of
\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}
, and denote
\alpha=\|\Lambda-\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
.
Let
(B^{1},\dots,B^{p})\in\mathfrak{orb}(G,n)
and
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{so}(n)
the Lie algebra they span.

•

Suppose that
\alpha\leq\lambda\Gamma(G,n)/2(2d+1)
.
Then the minimum in Equation (
9
) is not greater than
\lambda\Gamma(G,n)/2
if and only if there exists
O\in\mathrm{O}(n)
such that
O\mathfrak{h}O^{\bot}\subset\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
.
If so, let
\widehat{h}
be a minimizer.
Under the assumption that
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
and
\mathfrak{h}
have equal dimensions, there exists
O\in\mathrm{O}(n)
such that
\widehat{h}=O\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})O^{\top}
and
\|\Pi\big[\widehat{h}\big]-\Pi\big[\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})\big]\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\sqrt{2(2d+1)\alpha/\lambda}
.

•

Suppose that
\alpha\leq\lambda\Gamma(\mathrm{SO}(2),n,\omega_{\mathrm{max}})/2(2d+1)
for an integer
\omega_{\mathrm{max}}>0
and that the Lie algebras
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
and
\mathfrak{h}
are spanned by matrices whose spectra come from a primitive integral vector of coordinates at most
\omega_{\mathrm{max}}
.
If the minimum in Equation (
9
) is not greater than
\lambda\Gamma(G,n)/2
, then it is minimized for a Lie subalgebra
\widehat{h}
such that for all
A\in\widehat{h}
, there exists
B\in\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
and
O\in\mathrm{O}(n)
with
A=OBO^{\top}
and
\|A-B\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\sqrt{2(2d+1)\alpha/\lambda}
.

Proof.

We split the proof in two, following the statements of the lemma.

First point.

Let
\phi=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{p}\phi_{k}
be an almost-faithful representation of
G
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, with
B^{1},\dots,B^{p}
the corresponding Lie algebras, and denote
C_{j}=\mathrm{diag}\big(B^{k}_{j}\big)_{k=1}^{p}
for all
j\in[1\dots d]
.
Let
\mathfrak{h}
be the Lie algebra spanned by the
C_{j}
’s.
We are dealing with Equation (
9
), consisting of the minimization of
f^{\mathfrak{h}}\colon\mathrm{O}(n)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined below, together with its ideal counterpart, denoted
f^{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathcal{O}}
:

f^{\mathfrak{h}}(O)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\big\|\Lambda\big(OC_{j}O^{\top}\big)\big\|^{2}~~~~~\mathrm{and}~~~~~f^{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathcal{O}}(O)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\big\|\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}\big(OC_{j}O^{\top}\big)\big\|^{2}.

Using the notation
\alpha=\|\Lambda-\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
, we get that, for all
O\in\mathrm{O}(n)
,

\displaystyle|f^{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathcal{O}}(O)-f^{\mathfrak{h}}(O)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{d}\big(\big\|\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}\big(OC_{j}O^{\top}\big)\big\|+\big\|\Lambda\big(OC_{j}O^{\top}\big)\big\|\big)\big(\big\|\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}\big(OC_{j}O^{\top}\big)\big\|-\big\|\Lambda\big(OC_{j}O^{\top}\big)\big\|\big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{d}2\|\Lambda_{\mathcal{O}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\Lambda\|_{\mathrm{op}}\cdot\alpha\leq d\cdot 2\cdot\alpha.

That is to say,
\|f^{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathcal{O}}-f^{\mathfrak{h}}\|_{\infty}\leq 2d\alpha
.
Besides, we have derived in Equation (
46
) the lower bound

\lambda\big\|\Pi\big[\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})^{\bot}\big]\Pi\big[O\mathfrak{h}O^{\top}\big]\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leq f^{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathcal{O}}(O).

Now, two cases are to be distinguished.
If a certain
O\mathfrak{h}O^{\top}
belongs to
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
, then
f^{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathcal{O}}
admits zero as a minimum.
It follows that
\min f^{\mathfrak{h}}\leq\|f^{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathcal{O}}-f^{\mathfrak{h}}\|_{\infty}\leq 2d\alpha
.
Otherwise,
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
does not contain a conjugation of
\mathfrak{h}
and we deduce from the definition of
\Gamma(G,n)
, in Equation (
47
), that
\min f^{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathcal{O}}\geq\lambda\Gamma(G,n)
.
Hence,
\min f^{\mathfrak{h}}\geq\lambda\Gamma(G,n)-2d\alpha
, which is, by assumption on
\alpha
, greater or equal to
\lambda\Gamma(G,n)/2
, itself greater than
\alpha
.
We deduce the first statement.

Next, we suppose that
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
and
\widehat{h}
have the same dimension.
Let
\mathfrak{h}
be such that
\min f^{\mathfrak{h}}\leq\lambda\Gamma(G,n)/2
.
As shown above, this implies that
\min f^{\mathfrak{h}}\leq 2d\alpha
.
Let
\mathcal{O}_{*}
be a minimizer of
f_{\mathfrak{h}}
, and
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}=O_{*}\mathfrak{h}O_{*}^{\bot}
the corresponding Lie subalgebra.
We have

f^{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathcal{O}}(O_{*})\leq\min f^{\mathfrak{h}}+\|f^{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathcal{O}}-f^{\mathfrak{h}}\|_{\infty}\leq\alpha+2d\alpha.

By hypothesis, this quantity is lower or equal to
\lambda\Gamma(G,n)/2
.
We deduce that

\big\|\Pi\big[\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})^{\bot}\big]\Pi\big[O_{*}\mathfrak{h}O_{*}^{\top}\big]\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}f^{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathcal{O}}(O_{*})\leq\frac{(2d+1)\alpha}{\lambda}\leq\frac{1}{2}\Gamma(G,n).

Together with the definition of
\Gamma(G,n)
, we deduce that
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
contains a conjugation of
O_{*}\mathfrak{h}O_{*}^{\top}
. That is, there exists
O\in\mathrm{O}(n)
such that
O\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{O}^{\bot}\subset\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
.
Since the dimensions are equal, it follows that
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})=O\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{O}^{\bot}
.
For this same reason, we have that

\big\|\Pi\big[\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})^{\bot}\big]\Pi\big[O_{*}\mathfrak{h}O_{*}^{\top}\big]\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\|\Pi\big[\widehat{h}\big]-\Pi\big[\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})\big]\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2},

and we deduce the bound
\|\Pi\big[\widehat{h}\big]-\Pi\big[\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})\big]\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leq 2(2d+1)\alpha/\lambda
.

Second point.

We now suppose the assumptions of the second point of the lemma.
Since
\Gamma(\mathrm{SO}(2),n,\omega_{\mathrm{max}})\leq\Gamma(G,n)
, the conclusions of the first point are still valid.
Let
\mathfrak{h}
be such that
\min f^{\mathfrak{h}}\leq\lambda\Gamma(\mathrm{SO}(2),n,\omega_{\mathrm{min}})/2
.
Let
O_{*}
be a minimizer of
f^{\mathfrak{h}}
.
We still have

\big\|\Pi\big[\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})^{\bot}\big]\Pi\big[O_{*}\mathfrak{h}O_{*}^{\top}\big]\big\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leq\frac{(2d+1)\alpha}{\lambda}\leq\frac{1}{2}\Gamma(\mathrm{SO}(2),n,\omega_{\mathrm{max}}).

Thus, for any unit norm matrix
A\in O_{*}\mathfrak{h}O_{*}^{\top}
, there exists a matrix
B\in\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
such that

\|A-B\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\Gamma(\mathrm{SO}(2),n,\omega_{\mathrm{max}}).

By normalizing the matrix
B
, we obtain

\bigg\|A-\frac{B}{\|B\|}\bigg\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leq 2\|A-B\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leq\Gamma(\mathrm{SO}(2),n,\omega_{\mathrm{max}}).

We deduce, by definition of
\Gamma(\mathrm{SO}(2),n,\omega_{\mathrm{max}})
, that
A
and
B/\|B\|
admit the same spectrum.
Hence, there exists
O\in\mathrm{O}(n)
such that
B/\|B\|=OAO^{\top}
, as announced in the lemma.
∎

Note that this proof, and hence the lemma, are still valid with Equation (
10
) instead of (
9
).

5.3.3 Stability of
Step 4

We now study the last step of our algorithm.
Let
\mu_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\mu_{X}
denote respectively the uniform measure on
\mathcal{O}
and the empirical measure on
X
.
Moreover, let
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
be the Lie algebra output by
Step 3
or
Step 3
’.
The execution of
Step 4
consists in either choosing a point
x\in X
, forming the estimated orbit
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{x}
and computing the Hausdorff distance
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{H}}\big(X|\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{x}\big)
, or forming the estimated measure and computing
\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}
the Wasserstein distance
\mathrm{W}_{2}\big(\mu_{X},\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}\big)
.
We remind the reader that these objects are defined as

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{x}

\displaystyle=\big\{\exp(A)x\mid A\in\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}\big\}~~~~~~~\mathrm{and}~~~~~~~\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{x_{i}}}.

As one sees on this definition, and because of the matrix exponential, little inaccuracies in
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
may result in huge discrepancies between
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{x}
and
\mathcal{O}
.
To avoid this issue, we recall that, when restricted to conjugated skew-symmetric matrices, the matrix exponential is
1
-Lipschitz.

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
A\in\mathfrak{so}(n)
and
O\in\mathrm{O}(n)
.
Then for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, it holds that

\|\exp(tA)-\exp(tOAO^{\top})\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\|A-OAO^{\top}\|_{\mathrm{F}}.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can suppose that
A
is block diagonal.
There exists an integer
m\leq n/2
and
m
real numbers
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{m}
such that we can write

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\mathrm{diag}\big(L(\omega_{1}),\dots,L(\omega_{m})\big)

\displaystyle\mathrm{and}~~~~~\exp(tA)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{diag}\big(R(t\omega_{1}),\dots,R(t\omega_{m})\big),

with potentially zeros in the end, and where
L(\cdot)
and
R(\cdot)
are the
2\times 2
matrices defined in Example
2.2
.
We have the identity
R(t\omega_{1})=\cos(t\omega_{i})I+\sin(t\omega_{i})L(\omega_{i})
.
That is, we can write

\exp(tA)=\mathrm{diag}\big(\cos(t\omega_{1})I,\dots,\cos(t\omega_{m})I\big)+\mathrm{diag}\big(\sin(t\omega_{1})L(\omega_{1}),\dots,\sin(t\omega_{m})L(\omega_{m})\big).

Now, let
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}
denote the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
For any integer
i\in[0,n]
, and
j
the corresponding index in
[0,m]
, we deduce that

\|\exp(tA)e_{i}-O\exp(tA)O^{\top}e_{i}\|^{2}\leq(\cos(t\omega_{j})^{2}+\sin(t\omega_{j})^{2})\|A-B\|^{2}=\|A-B\|^{2}.

Summing this expression over all the
e_{i}
’s yields
\|\exp(tA)-\exp(tOAO^{\top})\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\|A-OAO^{\top}\|_{\mathrm{F}}
.
∎

We finally deduce a stability result for
Step 4
.
Our bound on the Hausdorff distance holds in general, whereas that for the Wasserstein distance is valid only under the assumption that
G
and
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
have the same dimension.
This is because we use in this paper the non-symmetric definition of the Hausdorff distance
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{H}}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{x}\mid\mathcal{O})
, which is small when
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{x}
is close to be a subset of
\mathcal{O}
.
On the contrary, the Wasserstein distance
\mathrm{W}_{2}\big(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}\big)
compares
\mu_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}
symetrically.

Proposition 5.19
.

Let
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an orbit,
X
a point cloud, and
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}\subset\mathfrak{so}(n)
a Lie subalgebra.
Let
\delta
be the maximal distance from
\mathcal{O}
to the origin.

•

Suppose that there exists
\beta\geq 0
such that for all
A\in\widehat{h}
, there exists
B\in\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})
and
O\in\mathrm{O}(n)
for which
A=OBO^{\top}
and
\|A-B\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\beta
.
Then for all
x\in X
, we have

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{H}}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{x}\mid\mathcal{O})\leq(\beta+1)\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{H}}(X\mid\mathcal{O})+\beta\delta.

•

Suppose that there exists
\beta\geq 0
and a matrix
O\in\mathrm{O}(n)
such that
\widehat{h}=O\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})O^{\top}
and
\|\Pi\big[\widehat{h}\big]-\Pi\big[\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{O})\big]\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\beta
.
Then it holds

\mathrm{W}_{2}\big(\mu_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}},\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\big)\leq\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{\mathcal{O}},\mu_{X})+\beta\delta.

Proof.

In this proof, we denote
\epsilon=\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{H}}(X\mid\mathcal{O})
.
Let
y\in\mathcal{O}
such that
\|x-y\|\leq\epsilon
.
We consider the intermediate sets

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{x}
